(age 42 years).389 In a series of 35 survivors of surgery followed up for a median duration of 23.4 years (range, 12.3 to 40.7 years), the actuarial survival rate was 97% at 10 years and 93% at both 20 years and 40 years after the initial operation. Reintervention in the RV outflow occurred in 97% of patients. Freedom from truncal valve replacement was 88% at 5 years, 85% at 10 years, and 70% at 20 years after the initial operation.391

*Indications for Operation.* Patients with truncal regurgitation usually present for repair at the time of neonatal correction, or within the first 10 to 15 years after repair389,390,392; therefore, presentation in the adult is unusual. Indications for repair or replacement are standard. Replacement of RV to PA conduit is indicated when there is significant stenosis or RV dysfunction as in repaired TOF with a RV-PA conduit. Significant PR following conduitless repair resulting in RV dilation may warrant pulmonary valve replacement.

#### **Double Outlet Right Ventricle**

*Definition.* The type of double outlet right ventricle (DORV) with subaortic VSD and pulmonary stenosis is discussed in this section (see Chapter 45). It is the most common form of DORV.

*Morphology.* DORV with subaortic VSD and pulmonary stenosis or atresia is managed surgically like tetralogy of Fallot. Late problems occur primarily in the reconstructed RVOT, as in tetralogy of Fallot. Additionally, there are concerns about the LVOT because of the subaortic conus and the more complex patch (or baffle) that is required to close the VSD and align the left ventricle with the aorta. The result is a complex tunnel-like LVOT. A combination of hypertrophy of the conus muscle and patch distortion lead to a greater chance of late subaortic stenosis than in tetralogy of Fallot. The obstruction is often morphologically complex, and repair may require replacing the VSD patch (baffle), with or without resection of conus muscle.

*Natural History.* Individuals with DORV rarely survive to adulthood without surgical repair. Those without pulmonary stenosis who do survive to adulthood have Eisenmenger physiology and are inoperable.393 Those with pulmonary stenosis who survive and are cyanotic from low pulmonary blood flow may be candidates for surgical repair, similar to individuals with unrepaired tetralogy of Fallot.394-397

*Technique of Operation.* In patients with postrepair LVOT obstruction, the initial surgical approach should be through an incision in the aortic root, with examination of the LVOT, looking retrograde through the aortic valve. If hypertrophied conus muscle is the primary cause of the obstruction, it can be resected effectively using this approach, similar to resection of typical isolated subaortic obstruction. If examination through the aortic root reveals more complex obstruction involving the previously placed patch (baffle), then a right ventriculotomy is made in the hood of the RV-to–pulmonary trunk conduit, or native RV infundibulum and the patch (baffle) is either revised or removed and replaced to relieve the obstruction.

## **Transposition of the Great Arteries with Ventricular Septal Defect**

*Definition.* The definition and morphology of TGA with VSD and LVOTO are discussed in Chapter 44.

*Natural History.* Individuals with TGA, VSD, and LVOTO rarely survive to adulthood without surgical repair. However, an occasional patient with prior palliation may survive to adulthood and present for surgery.398

*Technique of Operation.* Repair in infancy or childhood requires use of a RV-to–pulmonary trunk conduit in most cases. Thus, the usual late "tetralogy-like" RVOT problems are encountered. The intracardiac baffle created to close the VSD and align the left ventricle with the aorta at initial repair is even more complex than that in DORV. Thus, the same late LVOT concerns exist for the repaired adult TGA patient. Additionally, it is more likely that these transposition patients will be palliated with a shunt procedure in the neonatal period, so iatrogenic peripheral pulmonary artery stenosis is another concern.

Because of the anterior position of the aorta, the RV-to–pulmonary trunk conduit must often be placed directly in the midline behind the sternum, rather than positioned to the left side as is typical in tetralogy. The conduit may have been placed to the left or to the right of the ascending aorta. This conduit position causes two additional problems: (1) conduit compression between the mass of the heart and the sternum, leading to more frequent conduit reoperations, and (2) repeat sternotomy, which carries a greater risk of hemorrhagic complications than in tetralogy, with both the conduit and the anterior aorta vulnerable (see "Repeat Sternotomy" in Section I of this chapter).

## **Congenitally Corrected Transposition of the Great Arteries with Ventricular Septal Defect**

*Definition.* The definition of congenitally corrected transposition of the great arteries (CCTGA) with VSD and pulmonary stenosis or atresia is discussed in Chapter 47.

*Morphology.* CCTGA with VSD and pulmonary stenosis or atresia shares certain features with tetralogy of Fallot: surgical repair requires VSD closure and placement of a conduit from the heart to the pulmonary trunk. The definitive repair in infancy or childhood is either the "physiologic" repair or the "anatomic" repair. There are important sequelae in the adult after both operations. In the physiologic repair, VSD closure is straightforward, and the conduit is placed from the morphologic LV to the pulmonary trunk. The expected late conduit problems are a concern with this anomaly, just as with the other anomalies under discussion in this section. However, obstruction in the conduit may be better tolerated because the morphologic left ventricle and mitral valve are part of the pulmonary circulation.

An additional important late problem following physiologic repair is function of the tricuspid valve and morphologic right ventricle that are positioned in the systemic circulation. Premature failure of these structures is common at late follow-up after physiologic repair, as it is under any other circumstance when the right ventricle is left in the systemic circulation. (This subject is covered in more detail in Sections XII and XIII of this chapter.) When this circumstance is encountered after physiologic repair, the option of training the LV and subsequent reoperation to place the LV in the systemic circulation is an option. But this is a controversial choice, almost always inadvisable, because the LV already has a large ventriculotomy (raising concerns about its long-term function in the systemic circulation), and the operation would require placing a prosthetic valved conduit from the LV to the aorta. The alternative of cardiac transplantation is the better option.

In the anatomic repair in infancy or childhood, the "double switch" repair, the LV is aligned with the aorta via the VSD using a patch or baffle, a conduit is placed from the morphologic right ventricle to the pulmonary trunk, and an atrial switch is performed. Because of the position of the heart and aorta in CTGA, the conduit must often be positioned in the midline in this operation. The late concerns of conduit compression from the sternum are similar to those previously discussed for TGA. Additionally, in the double switch repair, atrial baffle complications, including intraatrial shunting, systemic or pulmonary venous obstruction, and sick sinus syndrome or junctional rhythm, can occur, similar to complications found after the Mustard or Senning operation for simple TGA.

*Natural History.* CCTGA with VSD and pulmonary stenosis may present as primary disease in adulthood, but in the current era, most patients with this form of CCTGA undergo surgery in infancy or childhood. This may involve initial palliation with a systemic-to–pulmonary artery shunt followed by later definitive repair, or initial definitive repair. After any kind of repair of CCTGA, complete heart block is frequent, occurring either at the time of initial repair or spontaneously at any time.

*Technique of Operation.* The surgical procedures for CCTGA and VSD are discussed in Chapter 47. In Connelly and colleagues' series of 52 patients with all forms of corrected transposition presenting in adulthood, 64% underwent definitive surgical repair.399 The most common procedure was the "physiologic" repair for patients with associated VSD and pulmonary stenosis, performed in 80% (20/25) of the definitive repairs. In six of these patients, the systemic tricuspid valve was replaced.

*Results.* In the series of 52 patients reported by Connelly and colleagues, early mortality was 5%. Two patients who had no systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation preoperatively developed severe regurgitation after repair, probably related to shift of the ventricular septum after left ventricular pressure was reduced. Exercise testing in survivors was subnormal, and there was no increase in systemic ventricular ejection fraction with exercise399.

In a series of 189 patients of all ages with all forms of corrected transposition, 152 had associated VSD and either pulmonary stenosis or atresia.400 Although mean age at surgery was 8 years, the upper end of the age range was 47 years, implying that at least some patients with VSD and pulmonary stenosis presented for initial repair in adulthood.

#### **Double Outlet Left Ventricle**

There are several forms of this extremely rare anomaly (see Chapter 46). In the repaired adult, in addition to the typical right-sided conduit problems, the LVOT must be monitored for late obstruction, just as in the other anomalies with left ventricular–to-aortic intracardiac patch or baffles. Commonly, repair involves complex closure of a VSD (with varying degrees of baffling required) and placement of a RV-to–pulmonary trunk conduit.

## **SECTION XII: TRANSPOSITION OF THE GREAT ARTERIES**

## **Definition**

The morphology, physiology, and natural history of TGA are described in Chapter 44.

## **Morphology**

There are several common morphologic variants of TGA: with intact ventricular septum (IVS); TGA with ventricular septal defect (VSD); TGA with aortic arch obstruction (AAO); and TGA with VSD and LVOTO. TGA with VSD and LVOTO is discussed in Section XI. The other variants require neonatal or infant surgical correction for the patient to survive.

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

#### **Presentation**

The adult presentation of these variants of TGA will almost exclusively be that of secondary congenital heart disease following early corrective surgery. The predominant corrective surgery for these variants between 1959 and about 1985 was some form of the atrial switch operation, either Senning or Mustard. The predominant corrective surgery between about 1985 and the present has been the arterial switch operation. Thus, currently, adults older than age 38 almost certainly will present with a background of atrial switch surgery, whereas those under age 38 are likely to present with a background of arterial switch surgery.401 These two populations present distinctly differently in adulthood.

A small but notable subset of the population with prior *atrial switch surgery* is made up of patients with failing systemic right ventricles who have undergone pulmonary artery banding in order to train the morphologic left ventricle in anticipation of atrial switch takedown and conversion to an arterial switch. These patients may enter adulthood at varying stages of LV training or after conversion to the arterial switch.

Adult survivors of the atrial switch may have a spectrum of conditions, including atrial arrhythmias (most commonly junctional rhythm with sick sinus syndrome [50% at 20 years] but also atrial fibrillation and flutter [35% at 20 years]), ventricular arrhythmias (9%), heart block, systemic and pulmonary venous obstruction within the atrial baffle, atrial septal defect, systemic (morphologic right) ventricular failure, systemic (tricuspid valve) atrioventricular valve regurgitation, and dynamic LV outflow tract obstruction.402,403

Systemic venous baffle obstruction within the superior limb leads to *superior vena caval syndrome:* swelling of the head and upper body, sometimes accompanied by headache or chylothorax. Systemic venous baffle obstruction in the inferior limb can lead to hepatic congestion, cirrhosis, ascites, and dependent peripheral edema. Baffle leaks will result in shunting, typically physiologically left to right, with pulmonary overcirculation; however, large defects may lead to mixing and systemic cyanosis or paradoxical embolus. Functional status of the systemic right ventricle also influences magnitude and direction of the shunt.

Pulmonary venous pathway baffle obstruction leads to pulmonary venous congestion and pulmonary hypertension. All the sequelae mentioned in the preceding paragraphs may already be well established in childhood, and commonly, pacemaker placement and revision of the atrial baffle, due to either leak or obstruction, may have already been performed before adulthood is reached. These sequelae have a substantial effect on quality of life and survival.404-410 In a recent multicenter retrospective cohort study in parents over age 18, 24% had received a pacemaker and a further 3.3% an ICD. 17% had a baffle intervention since switch and 1% had undergone a tricuspid valve operation (Table 54.19).411

*CHF*, congestive heart failure; *ICD*, implantable cardioverter-defibrillator; *VSD*, ventricular septal defect.

These multiple sequelae notwithstanding, systemic right heart function (RV dysfunction, tricuspid valve regurgitation, or both) and sudden death due to arrhythmia are the major issue in adults being followed with an atrial switch operation. Some degree of systemic right heart dysfunction is present in most patients by the time they reach adulthood. In a recent multicenter study of 1,168 patients over 18, followed for a median of 9.2 years, with primary endpoints of death, transplantation or mechanical circulatory support, 8.8% reached that point at 10 years.411 However in the same study it was also noted that many older patients with systemic right ventricle remained stable with less than 20% of the total cohort affected by admission for heart failure, ventricular arrhythmia or severe RV dysfunction (Fig. 54.38).

Patients with prior *arterial switch* present in adulthood with fewer sequelae than do those with prior atrial switch. Some studies indicate that patients are physiologically and functionally on par with the age-matched normal population.412 One study of 65 patients at a median age of 19 years shows, however, that exercise capacity was 73% of normal. As more of those patients reach adulthood, we are learning that there will be sequelae but that they are distinctly different, reflecting the differences between the two operations. In a study of 65 patients by Tobler and colleagues, 17% (12/65) of adults had at least one important cardiac lesion.413 Arrhythmias accounted for 7%, with atrial flutter in three patients, sick sinus syndrome in one, and ventricular tachycardia in one. Structural lesions accounted for the other 10%, with RV dysfunction in three patients, severe pulmonary regurgitation in two, and LV dysfunction, severe tricuspid regurgitation, and LV outflow tract obstruction in one each. They also noted aortic root dilation (.36 mm diameter) in 31%, but no intervention was required. Aortic regurgitation occurred in 52%; however, it was mild or less in all. There were no documented coronary artery lesions in this series; however, in other series coronary lesions were noted in 7% of cases at late follow-up.414

Other arterial switch series emphasize RVOT obstruction, coronary insufficiency and its sequelae, and aortic root dilation and aortic regurgitation.401,415-418 RVOT obstruction occurs in 5% to 15% of patients and tends to develop within several years of the arterial switch; rarely will this become a new problem late postoperatively. Most patients with serious stenosis will undergo revision of the RVOT during childhood, and some will require multiple revisions. This may ultimately result in important pulmonary regurgitation or need for a prosthetic valve or valved conduit. As adults, these patients require further surgical management of the RVOT for problems related to right-sided conduit failure.

Among the potential late complications after the arterial switch operation, coronary artery issues represent the greatest risk for mortality. Coronary artery problems develop late in up to 8% of patients after arterial switch particularly those with intramural coronary arteries.401 Some of these patients may be asymptomatic, and will be detected by abnormal exercise ECG or elective coronary imaging. In a study of 993 adult patients late after arterial switch, 9% had suspected ischemia, and only 0.4% underwent coronary interventions despite frequent findings of angulation (40%), kinking (34%), or stenosis (4%).418a. A study of coronary CT angiography in 50 asymptomatic patients revealed coronary abnormalities in 24%.418b Given the low incidence of symptomatic lesions, the role of routine coronary CT angiography in follow-up has not been established.

In 2019 van der Palen and colleagues studied neoaortic growth after the arterial switch for TGA and demonstrated that after proportional growth in childhood the root, anulus and STJ dimensions all continue to increase in adulthood without stabilization of dimensions. They also found that neoaortic regurgitation continues to develop over time and correlates with root dimensions. Risk factors for aortic regurgitation include complete TGA anatomy and rate of neoaortic growth.419 Nakayama and colleagues from

![](_page_63_Figure_7.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.38** Kaplan-Meier Curves for Endpoint-Free Survival After Sentinel Events Modified Kaplan-Meier style curves showing endpoint-free survival after first incidence of pacemaker placement (red), atrial arrhythmia (blue), ventricular arrhythmia (gray), or heart failure admission (purple) for individuals experiencing these events. Shaded regions represent corresponding 95% CIs. Curves are superimposed and not concurrent or mutually exclusive. (From Broberg CS, van Dissel A, Minnier J, et al. Long-Term outcomes after atrial switch operation for transposition of the great arteries. *J Am Coll Cardiol*. 2022;80:951-963) 411

Tokyo reported outcomes of 469 hospital survivors of the arterial switch who had more than moderate aortic regurgitation in 8.6% at 19 years median follow-up, including 17 patients (3.6%) undergoing aortic surgery, mostly valve replacement (Fig. 54.39).420

Other long-term outcomes have also more recently been represented by Fricke and colleagues, with a wide range of valvar reoperation included but the main late lesion requiring adult surgery again appears to be the neoaortic valve, on follow-up of over 800 arterial switches. They conclude that clinically significant aortic regurgitation or neoaortic valve replacement occurred in 15% of survivors by 25 years.421

Finally, most patients following the atrial switch operation would now be considered too old for LV retraining and conversion to an arterial switch; and a severely failing systemic right ventricle is an indication for heart transplantation, which has good outcome relative to other congenital cardiac indications (see Chapter 21).

LV dysfunction may develop in patients with a history of initial atrial switch followed by late pulmonary trunk banding for LV training and conversion to arterial switch. The dysfunction is currently poorly understood, but is probably related to inadequacy of the LV training process in most cases. Coronary artery obstruction is usually not implicated. All patients with a history of LV dysfunction and late conversion to the arterial switch should undergo frequent evaluation to assess LV function.

#### **Diagnosis**

Evaluation of the adult with *prior atrial switch surgery* may include *electrocardiogram* (ECG), echocardiography, magnetic resonance imaging (MRI), computed tomography (CT), and cardiac catheterization. ECG documents baseline rhythm, status of sinoatrial and atrioventricular nodes, and presence of ventricular ectopy. Holter monitoring is commonly helpful if any rhythm disturbance is suspected. Exercise testing at regular intervals may help to determine subclinical deterioration, particularly in secondary patients, and demonstrate response to treatments.

*Echocardiography* is the most important tool in assessing the status of both systemic venous limbs of the atrial baffle, pulmonary venous pathway, baffle leakage, systemic RV function, and systemic tricuspid valve function. Transesophageal echocardiography may enhance the findings of surface studies. Nevertheless, it is acknowledged that echocardiography has limited ability to assess RV function.422,423

MRI and CT provide superior imaging information about the atrial baffle and its pathways and the great arteries and extracardiac central veins and can provide additional valuable information about the systemic RV and tricuspid valve. In particular, MRI can be used to calculate RV mass and volume, ejection fraction, and tricuspid regurgitant fraction. Myocardial delayed enhancement can evaluate RV myocardial viability.424

*Cardiac catheterization* is critical in the evaluation. Angiography is the standard for imaging the coronary arteries. A complete hemodynamic evaluation, and in particular measurement of systemic RV end-diastolic pressure, provides crucial information for managing the systemic right heart.

Electrical, morphologic, and hemodynamic data obtained from these studies are used to determine management of the TGA patient with a systemic right ventricle. Management options include heart failure and rhythm medications, catheter interventions for atrial baffle leaks, venous obstructions, pacemakers and arrhythmia ablations, and for outflow tract obstructions (LV to PA pathway).

Surgical options prior to consideration for cardiac transplantation will include biatrial Maze procedure in addition to tricuspid valve repair or replacement, residual VSD closure and (partial) relief of LV outflow obstruction leaving LV pressure half systemic. Epicardial pacing options, including biventricular pacing, should also be considered.

![](_page_64_Figure_14.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.39** Kaplan-Meier curves showing (A) more than moderate neoaortic regurgitation (AR)–free survival after arterial switch operation and (B) neoaortic valve surgery-free survival after arterial switch operation. Reproduced from Nakayama Y, Shinkawa T, Matsumura G, Hoki R, Kobayashi K, Niinami H. Late neo-aortic valve regurgitation long after arterial switch operation. *Ann Thorac Surg*. 2019;108(4):1210- 1216. doi:10.1016/j.athoracsur.2019.04.009

Routine evaluation in the adult with *prior arterial switch* includes ECG and echocardiography. CT or MR or coronary angiography should also be considered if the original operative note from the arterial switch operation indicates an unusual coronary artery pattern or difficulty with coronary artery translocation. ECG is most helpful in showing ischemic changes either at rest or with exercise; however, it will also document atrial and ventricular arrhythmias.

Echocardiography assesses function of all four valves (with particular focus on the semilunar valves), ventricular function, and size of the aortic root. The coronary arteries cannot be fully assessed by echocardiography, but indirect evidence of coronary insufficiency, such as ventricular wall motion abnormalities and mitral regurgitation, can be.

MRI and CT with angiography are also used selectively, for example, when more precise imaging is needed than can be provided by echocardiography (for serial assessment of aortic root size), when qualitative functional valve assessment is needed (for serial assessment of aortic regurgitation), or when functional myocardial evaluation is needed (magnetic resonance myocardial delayed enhancement).

Electrophysiologic evaluation is indicated in all adults with TGA, regardless of the type of surgery, if clinical course, ECG, or Holter monitoring suggests important atrial or ventricular rhythm disturbance.

## **Natural History**

Many adults with *prior atrial switch surgery* survive to adulthood. Twenty-five-year survival is 65%, and is even better (80%) if TGA is not complicated by VSD.425 Late mortality risk after the first 5 years following surgery appears to be linear at 0.5% per year.402 Studies show a concerning trend in measures of systemic ventricular function over time, with both cardiac index and stroke volume index inversely related to age (Figs. 54.40 and 54.41).426 Of the 66 late deaths represented in a recent adult cohort, the average age

Survival for those who have reached adulthood after the *arterial switch* remains over 95% but reoperations on the neoaortic root may impact on these curves in the next decade **(**Fig. 54.42).

## **Technique of Operation**

## **After Atrial Switch Repair**

Several surgical options are available for superior and inferior vena caval baffle limb obstruction. The preferred approach involves direct revision of the systemic venous component of the baffle itself. This involves cardiopulmonary bypass (CPB) using bicaval venous cannulation and aortic clamping with cardioplegia administration. The systemic venous component of the baffle is exposed using an incision into the morphologic right atrial free wall, which is part of the pulmonary venous pathway of the baffle. Patch augmentation of the affected limb of the systemic venous baffle is then performed.

An alternative option for superior limb obstruction is to perform a bidirectional superior cavopulmonary anastomosis. This option may be preferred if there is longstanding long-segment occlusion of the superior limb or very complex nonocclusive obstruction. This option is contraindicated if pulmonary vascular resistance is elevated above about 3 Wood units.

Interventional catheter techniques using balloon dilation and stent has been successful in relieving most of the obstruction in recent years so that surgical intervention is now rarely indicated.

Pulmonary venous baffle obstruction almost always occurs at the midpoint of the pathway as it crosses the plane of the (normal) atrial septum and wraps around the systemic venous component of the baffle. Surgical approach involves CPB as described previously for systemic venous baffle obstruction. Patch augmentation of the external wall of the morphologic right atrium (which is part of the pulmonary venous pathway), with the patch

![](_page_66_Figure_2.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.42** Scatter plot of Age vs. Initial Visit and Last Contact. Reproduced from Broberg CS, van Dissel A, Minnier J, et al. Long-Term outcomes after atrial switch operation for transposition of the great arteries. *J Am Coll Cardiol.* 2022;80:951-963.

extending down onto the right pulmonary veins, usually relieves the obstruction.

Operation for baffle leakage is performed using the same exposure described previously for systemic venous baffle obstruction*.* The leak may be at the suture line or through multiple atrial muscle trabeculations remote from the patch suture line. After opening the morphologic right atrial wall and entering the chamber that accepts pulmonary venous blood, suture line leaks are usually obvious and are best closed with additional patch material. Muscular trabeculation leaks are best assessed by performing the additional step of opening the chamber that accepts systemic venous blood and examining the internal surface of the systemic venous pathway. Direct suture closure or patching of all communications from this vantage point is then carried out.

Interventional catheter techniques using ASD devices have become the standard option for baffle leaks, in particular they must be closed in the presence of endocardial pacing leads due to the possibility of paradoxical embolism and risk of stroke, and preferably before any redo conduit surgery.

Subvalvar LV-to–pulmonary trunk obstruction is approached using CPB with bicaval venous cannulation and aortic clamping with administration of cardioplegia. Access to the subvalvar obstruction is through an incision in the pulmonary trunk, with retrograde exposure through the pulmonary valve. Muscular and fibromuscular resection is performed using the same techniques used for subaortic obstruction (see Chapter 50). The resection should be conservative aiming to achieve an LV pressure half systemic, so the septum does not shift too far and aggravate tricuspid valve regurgitation.

Tricuspid valve regurgitation in atrial switch patients used to be treated conservatively, but data published in 2005 indicated that timely TVR improves life expectancy.427 Very occasionally, a tethered septal leaflet will be an isolated cause of regurgitation in a patient with a history of atrial switch with concomitant closure of a VSD. The tethered leaflet is iatrogenic. If systolic RV function is normal, repair of the tricuspid valve with partial closure of the septal anterior commissure at the site of tethering may be effective. More complex etiologies of tricuspid regurgitation in the setting of normal RV function are best managed using tricuspid valve replacement. Both bioprosthetic and mechanical valves are appropriate options. The decision is based on the typical advantages and disadvantages of each type of prosthesis.

Atrial Maze and epicardial biventricular pacing should be performed concurrently with TVR if indicated.

#### **After Arterial Switch Repair**

Repair of RVOT problems after the arterial switch can usually be managed using normothermic beating heart CPB with a single venous cannula in the right atrium, once an on-table bubble study has confirmed there is no residual septal defect. Because of the Lecompte maneuver, access to the entire RVOT, including subvalvar, valvar, and supravalvar areas, and branch pulmonary arteries, is excellent. However this also makes it prone to injury during reentry. Patch augmentation of obstructive lesions in MPA or branches is effective. Valvar obstruction may be managed by valvuloplasty, transanular patch, or valve replacement. Transanular patching will result in some degree of pulmonary regurgitation. Valve options include bioprosthetic valves and pulmonary and aortic allograft valved conduits. Isolated subvalvar obstruction can be resected, working retrogradely through the pulmonary valve, or by a longitudinal infundibular incision with resection or infundibular patching. Preoperative coronary imaging is important here to rule out a major coronary crossing the RVOT and allow planning for RV-PA conduit insertion as an alternative.

Coronary artery obstruction and aortic valve and root problems are managed with techniques described in Chapters 9 and 12. Surgical access to the aortic root, valve, and coronary arteries is more difficult than usual because of their posterior position behind the pulmonary trunk following the Lecompte maneuver. To access the aortic root the pulmonary trunk or the right pulmonary origin needs to be transected and mobilized. CTCA imaging is helpful here to prevent damage to the previous transferred coronaries origins during this dissection. Coronary ostial kinking may be managed by ostial reimplantation, root remodeling or by ostial patching if there is intrinsic stenosis.

In the case of neoaortic regurgitation and a dilated root the preferred option is a bioprosthetic or mechanical root replacement or a valve sparing root replacement if the valve is reasonably competent. Simple AVR has resulted in this situation has resulted in the need for a difficult third operation as the neoaortic root continues to enlarge and then may distort the coronary origins or flatten the pulmonary artery branches straddling in front of it.  $^{\rm 420}$ 

The classically described *Maze procedure* is performed in patients with the arterial switch and atrial fibrillation. An appropriately modified Maze procedure is used when there is an atrial baffle in place (see Chapter 15), but involves removal of all the old material so that the cryotherapy lines are complete, before the new patches are inserted.

Transplantation is performed with the appropriate arterial and venous anastomotic adjustments (see Chapter 21).

#### **Results**

Studies of the outcomes of late surgery in adult TGA patients are now being published, albeit in relatively small numbers and with a variety of morphologies, prior operations and comorbidities. This makes it difficult to accurately predict mortality and it may be best to refer to the ACHD genetic scores such as the PEACH Score<sup>149</sup> for the most objective guidance.

The mortality for adults undergoing conversion from atrial to arterial switch was 20% or higher in older papers  $^{428}$  and the much lower 5% mortality after transplantation may well be the reason this operation is no longer commonly reported, along with the increasing age of these patients.

Late reoperation for neoaortic regurgitation and root dilation after the arterial switch is generally low risk. In a report from Tokyo, 17 patients underwent surgery at a median age of 16.5 years with 100% early survival, 3 required a further reoperation and there were 2 late deaths, both with infectious endocarditis, giving an actuarial survival at 10 and 20 years after neo aortic valve surgery of 92% and 79% (Fig. 54.43).

Myers and colleagues reported a series of 34 children and young adults undergoing a valve sparing root procedure for moderate or severe AR, which included 5 with either simple or congenitally corrected TGA. There was 100% early and late survival but reoperations were needed in 5 due to aortic leaflet prolapse and undersizing of the reimplantation graft. They concluded that valve sparing root replacement (VSRR) techniques are applicable but need modification for congenital heart patients, to allow for the large size of their anulus and valve leaflets. 429

Other case reports are emerging with successful outcomes of the VSRR in adults with neoaortic regurgitation after both simple arterial switch and also double switch operations for congenitally corrected  $TGA.^{430,431}$ 

## **Indications for Operation**

#### **Rhythm Disorders**

In all adult patients with transposition, regardless of their surgical history, arrhythmias may require intervention. Indications for pacemaker insertion include sick sinus syndrome, symptomatic bradycardia, and selected patients with a stable junctional rhythm that does not meet bradycardia criteria. Although the

![](_page_67_Figure_15.jpeg)

![](_page_67_Figure_16.jpeg)

• Figure 54.43 Kaplan-Meier curves showing (A) survival after neoaortic valve surgery and (B) reoperation-free survival after neo-aortic valve surgery. Reproduced from Nakayama Y, Shinkawa T, Matsumura G, Hoki R, Kobayashi K, Niinami H. Late neo-aortic valve regurgitation long after arterial switch operation. *Ann Thorac Surg.* 2019;108:1210-1216.

atrial morphology may be altered, particularly in atrial switch patients, transvenous pacing systems can usually be placed. Surgical placement of epicardial pacing systems remains an option for those with unusual morphology, intracardiac shunts, or occluded central veins, and for those requiring biventricular pacing. Sudden death is described in adult atrial switch patients, mostly related to ventricular tachycardia and fibrillation.432 Placing an internal cardioverter-defibrillator (ICD) is indicated if a sudden death episode is documented or electrophysiologic evaluation demonstrates inducible ventricular tachycardia or fibrillation. The Maze procedure, or appropriate modifications if atrial structures are altered, may be indicated for any adult patient with TGA if atrial fibrillation or flutter is present and other cardiac surgery is planned.

#### **After Atrial Switch Repair**

Both surgical and catheter-based interventional procedures are available for residual and recurrent lesions in adults with prior atrial switch surgery. Intervention for the structural lesions described in the following text is indicated if the lesions cause important symptoms or if hemodynamic alterations caused by these lesions meet standard criteria for intervention.

Catheter-based dilation or stenting is the preferred method for treating symptomatic superior caval, inferior caval, and pulmonary venous pathway obstruction, and for baffle leaks. It is also an option for supravalvar pulmonary trunk and branch pulmonary artery stenosis, but the subpulmonary left ventricle may tolerate the stenosis well and a conservative approach may be preferable.

Surgical intervention for LV outflow tract obstruction in atrial switch patients is indicated only if LV pressure becomes suprasystemic or symptoms are attributable to the obstruction. Moderate or even severe obstruction, as judged by pressure gradient, may not affect LV function. Additionally, the argument has been made that some elevation of LV pressure may be beneficial, keeping the ventricular septum from bowing away from the right ventricle, thereby stabilizing the subvalvar mechanism of the tricuspid valve and reducing development of systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation.

Surgical intervention may be indicated in selected cases of moderate or severe systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation, but before systemic RV function has deteriorated. Repair of the tricuspid valve working under systemic loading conditions remains a challenge. In some cases, tricuspid valve regurgitation is associated with a dilated and poorly functioning systemic right ventricle. Catheter studies with wedge pressures or RVEDP may help to determine which patients will still benefit from TVR and which are too high risk and better to be referred for transplantation. Important atrial or ventricular arrhythmias, morphologic LV dysfunction, and mitral valve dysfunction all strongly favor moving toward transplantation.

## **After Arterial Switch Repair**

Indications for surgery in adults with prior arterial switch and RVOT lesions are the same as those for tetralogy of Fallot (see Section XI of this chapter). Indications for coronary artery surgery are similar to those in patients with arteriosclerotic disease (see Chapter 9) and include signs and symptoms of myocardial ischemia and coronary artery luminal narrowing of 50% or greater.

Risk of aortic dissection and rupture associated with aortic dilation after the arterial switch is very low and reoperation on this area is not as straightforward as for other congenital adult patients, thus it is not yet clear what the indications for root replacement are. As the root approaches 6cm the operation may become more difficult, hence a reason to proceed at 5.5cm, especially if root dilation is obstructing the straddling pulmonary arteries. If aortic valve function is normal, a valve-sparing root replacement should be considered before significant AR develops. If root dilation is accompanied by more than mild aortic regurgitation, root replacement with aortic valve replacement, or aortic valve repair, is indicated.

The likely low risk of late rupture or dissection with moderate isolated aortic root and/or ascending aortic dilation after the neonatal arterial switch is supported by a report from Stulak and colleagues in adults with conotruncal defects and dilated aortas323. Of 63 patients having simple AVR with moderate dilation of their aortic root, none required root surgery over 10 years of follow-up. There were no late aortic dissections.

## **SECTION XIII: CONGENITALLY CORRECTED TRANSPOSITION OF THE GREAT ARTERIES**

## **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of congenitally corrected transposition of the great arteries (corrected transposition, CCTGA) are described in Chapter 47. CCTGA with pulmonary stenosis or atresia and ventricular septal defect (VSD), the single most common form, is discussed in detail in Section XI of this chapter. All other forms are discussed in this section.

## **Morphology**

CCTGA is a rare anomaly and thus is not often found in adults. It may present in adulthood as newly diagnosed disease, previously diagnosed disease with benign physiology, or secondary disease with a history of previous surgery. Presentation will be variable because all but 1% to 2% of patients have one or more major associated structural cardiac anomalies, including pulmonary stenosis, VSD, and systemic atrioventricular (AV) valve abnormalities, and these largely determine the nature of presentation. Even the rare patient without associated structural anomalies will eventually develop problems with conduction or with systemic RV function.

There are several morphologic variants of CCTGA:

- Without structural anomalies
- With VSD
- With pulmonary stenosis or atresia
- With Ebsteinoid tricuspid valve
- With situs inversus
- With combinations of the above

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

#### **Presentation**

Initial presentation may be spontaneous heart block, which is well documented to occur at a rate of about 2% per year in CCTGA. Patients with a prior history of VSD closure or those without structural abnormalities or any prior surgery, may present with failure of the systemic right ventricle causing the classic left heart signs and symptoms. If a prior prosthetic valve has been placed in the tricuspid position it may stenose if it is a bioprosthesis or be outgrown, and the patient presents with the typical signs of left inlet valve stenosis. Atrial tachycardias may develop in adulthood as a hallmark of impending systemic ventricular failure. If there has been pulmonary valve surgery or replacement, conduit failure or regurgitation may eventually compromise the morphologic LV, although many patients with moderate or even severe stenosis of the pulmonary valve have good function of their LV and no symptoms.

A past history of double switch surgery may present with late complications related to the coronaries or aorta due to the arterial transfer, and/or the late baffle obstructions sometimes found after the atrial switch (see Section XII, "Transposition of the Great Arteries," earlier in this chapter). Signs and symptoms of systemic RV failure, systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation, and complete heart block are the same as in any patient with left-sided heart failure or heart block and therefore are nonspecific for CTGA. Evaluation will lead to the diagnosis.

#### **Diagnosis**

The *electrocardiogram* will show definitive evidence of ventricular inversion and of situs inversus, if present. The chest *radiogram* is not definitive, but may show abnormal contour of the ventricular shadow, a narrowed superior mediastinal shadow due to the position of the great arteries, and abnormal cardiac position such as mesocardia or dextrocardia. The *echocardiogram* is diagnostic, identifying AV and ventriculoarterial discordant connections, as well as the major associated anomalies such as VSD, pulmonary stenosis, and systemic tricuspid valve structural abnormalities and function.434 Apical displacement of the tricuspid valve is easily identified, and complexity of pulmonary stenosis, including valvar and subvalvar components, can usually be determined. Quantitative assessment of systemic RV function is often difficult by echocardiography. *Magnetic resonance imaging* (MRI) is used to assess systemic RV function and volume and to quantify tricuspid valve regurgitation. It is particularly useful in cases of situs inversus or dextrocardia where standard echocardiographic views need to be inverted and where position of structures such as the aortic valve can be a long way from the usual. It allows the best imaging of all the regional areas of both ventricles for perfusion and contractility assessments. *Cardiac catheterization* is often helpful in assessing systemic RV function and RV end diastolic pressure and assessment of the degree of LV outflow obstruction. CT is useful in preoperative planning for cannulation and approach to the various cardiac chambers and lesions, and for safe resternotomy in reoperative cases.

## **Natural History**

The undiagnosed asymptomatic adult may present incidentally during evaluation for some other problem.435,436 The asymptomatic adult with known unrepaired CCTGA may be referred to the adult congenital cardiac clinic simply because there is a known diagnosis. Most commonly, the unrepaired patient without associated structural anomalies, or with mild associated structural anomalies, will present in the fourth or fifth decade of life with signs and symptoms of systemic RV failure, systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation, or arrythmias.435,437 In one single-institution study, by age 50 years, 66% of patients were in heart failure, but none died over a 10-year observation period thereafter.437 In a multiinstitutional longitudinal study, 25% of patients with CCTGA and no other structural anomalies presented with signs and symptoms of heart failure by age 45 years. If associated structural anomalies were present, heart failure by age 45 years increased to 67%.438

Patients presenting in adulthood without prior surgery rarely have severe pulmonary stenosis, large VSDs, or severely abnormal systemic tricuspid valves, because the natural history of these associated anomalies rarely allows patients to reach adulthood in a healthy state. Isolated moderate or moderate to severe pulmonary stenosis may not result in LV dysfunction or mitral regurgitation because the left ventricle will be working under afterload conditions it can tolerate well.

## **Technique of Operation**

The surgical techniques recommended in this section—direct repair of associated anomalies and the double switch operation are described in Chapter 47. Other techniques used to repair sequelae related to atrial and arterial switches are described in Chapters 44. General advice for reoperations on complex adult congenital patients is particularly valuable for this rare group of patients and may be found within the *Overview Section* of this section and in Chapter 5.

#### **Results**

Early surgical mortality in adults after repair of the associated anomalies found with CCTGA is not well documented but is likely very low, similar to that associated with repair of these anomalies in younger patients. In a series of 44 patients (mean age 44 years) presenting in adulthood for surgery to correct various associated structural anomalies (atrial septal defect, VSD, severe aortic regurgitation, severe tricuspid regurgitation, severe mitral regurgitation, severe pulmonary stenosis), there were no early deaths (CL 0%–4.2%).435 Four patients who underwent tricuspid valve replacement for severe regurgitation required cardiac transplantation at a mean interval of 56 months after initial surgery. In another series, early mortality for VSD closure and placement of a left ventricular–to–pulmonary trunk conduit in adults was 5.8% (3/52; CL 2.6%–11%).399 Isolated tricuspid valve replacement can be performed with low mortality. Mongeon and colleagues reported no early deaths (CL 0%–4.0%) in 46 patients (mean age 34 years) and actuarial survival of 69% at 10 years.439 Patients with initial RV ejection fraction greater than 45% had better 10-year survival than those with lower ejection fraction, 94% versus 55%.

Coronary artery bypass grafting for arteriosclerotic coronary artery disease has been reported in a 47-year-old diabetic adult with CTGA.440

The elective double switch in adult patients with naturally prepared left ventricles has a low early mortality comparable to that for children, around 2%. LV retraining in the adult has become rarer as the series of double switches is now demonstrating some late LV dysfunction and the risk of this appears age-related. Neoaortic valve regurgitation is also a concern, if the valve trunk has had to be banded, and so heart transplantation is often seen as a less risky alternative, if the right ventricle is failing.

## **Indications for Operation**

Surgery may be indicated in adults with unrepaired CCTGA if additional structural anomalies are present and are causing symptoms, or if there are no additional structural anomalies but the systemic right ventricle is failing. Options for surgery if structural anomalies are present include isolated surgical repair of the anomaly or anomalies causing the symptoms, double switch with concomitant management of the associated anomaly or anomalies, and cardiac transplantation.

Criteria for isolated VSD closure, pulmonary stenosis repair, or systemic tricuspid valve repair or replacement for regurgitation are generally similar to the criteria used for similar isolated anomalies in otherwise normal hearts; however, there are some notable modifications. The status of the systemic right ventricle must be considered carefully when a small VSD is present. The already afterload-strained ventricle may not compensate for even a small volume load; thus, small VSDs may require closure if the systemic right ventricle is dilated or dysfunctional. The same argument can be used when assessing systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation. Tricuspid valve repair is difficult in the systemic tricuspid valve, especially if there are structural valve abnormalities. Valve replacement is usually the procedure of choice. Pulmonary stenosis, on the other hand, may be better tolerated with a morphologic left ventricle in the pulmonary circulation, and surgical intervention may not be necessary for moderate or even moderate to severe obstruction.

The double switch is an option that should be considered in selected cases. It is an attractive option if the left ventricle is already prepared for a systemic workload due to a combination of VSD and pulmonary or subpulmonary stenosis (see Section XI of this chapter). The double switch is less attractive when the left ventricle is unprepared for the systemic workload, as is the case when there are no, or minor, associated structural anomalies. The double switch in this setting will require preparatory left ventricular training (see Chapter 47).

In unrepaired adults without structural anomalies there is no indication for surgery if systemic RV function is stable and the tricuspid valve does not have significant regurgitation. Many of these adults can remain reasonably well without interventions, albeit with careful follow-up.

In unrepaired adults without associated structural anomalies who have systemic RV failure resistant to treatment along with catheter hemodynamics which indicate the right ventricle is beyond recovery, heart transplantation is the lower risk option. However, there may be anatomic variants which make transplant very difficult along with underlying organ damage that increases mortality of a long bypass run, and this may make the surgeon consider other simpler options such as pulmonary artery banding to increase LV pressure, splint the septum and which may also improve tricuspid regurgitation. If symptoms are due to severe pulmonary stenosis and the left ventricle is failing, then simple relief of the stenosis may help, either with a valvotomy or an LV to pulmonary artery conduit. Anatomic repair requires the pulmonary valve to become the neoaortic aortic so the Double Switch would only be possible if the obstruction to the LVOT was entirely subvalvar and could be resected without neoaortic regurgitation. This would need to be assessed carefully on preoperative TEE.

In selected unrepaired adults without associated structural anomalies who have important tricuspid regurgitation but preserved RV function (ejection fraction . 45%), tricuspid valve replacement may be considered instead of the double switch or transplantation.439 There is a single case report of a 64-year-old patient who presented with complete heart block and severe tricuspid valve regurgitation who underwent placement of a sequential AV pacemaker, resulting in markedly improved tricuspid valve function.441

In adults with previous surgery for CCTGA in whom the systemic ventricle remains the morphologic right ventricle, indications for surgery and surgical options are generally the same as for unrepaired patients. Patients with left ventricle–to–pulmonary trunk conduits in place will eventually require conduit replacement for conduit failure. Moderate and moderate to severe conduit obstruction may be well tolerated when the left ventricle is in the pulmonary circulation, and in fact some elevation of left ventricular pressure may be beneficial, helping to stabilize the ventricular septum in midposition, thereby preventing systemic tricuspid valve regurgitation. When severe conduit obstruction is present and surgery is necessary, the fact that the left ventricle is prepared for the systemic circulation may make the double switch seem attractive; however, presence of the left ventriculotomy (from the conduit) and lack of a straightforward option for creating left ventricle to aortic continuity make the double switch inadvisable in most cases.

In adults with previous double switch surgery for CCTGA, indications for surgery are a combination of the indications for surgery in patients with simple transposition and prior atrial switch and in patients with simple transposition and prior arterial switch. (See Section XII, Transposition of the Great Arteries, earlier in this chapter.)

#### **SECTION XIV: EBSTEIN ANOMALY**

## **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of Ebstein anomaly are described in Chapter 48. Of adults presenting with congenital heart disease, Ebstein anomaly accounts for only 1% of all congenital heart disease,442 and about 50% of those born with this anomaly do not survive to adulthood, especially those with a critical neonatal presentation.443,444 About 10% of patients will present for the first time in adulthood.445 Reflecting the wide spectrum of physiologic derangement, the presentation in adults may be newly diagnosed primary disease, previously diagnosed primary disease with benign physiology, or secondary disease following earlier surgery. Some patients with severe Ebstein anomaly enter a single-ventricle surgical management strategy in childhood. They will not be discussed in this section.

## **Morphology**

A hallmark of Ebstein anomaly is its wide spectrum of morphologic deformity of the tricuspid valve, and of the right ventricle, and the associated wide spectrum of physiologic derangement. One classification was developed by Carpentier into categories ABC and D depending on the degree of displacement of the tricuspid valve septal leaflet down into the right ventricle. Unique to adults are the changes to the left ventricular function and cardiac output over time, due to chronic RV low output and ventricularventricular interactions.446

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

#### **Presentation**

Newly diagnosed adults commonly present with palpitations, dyspnea, or fatigue following new onset of atrial tachyarrhythmias, such as atrial flutter, atrial fibrillation, or reentrant tachycardia through an accessory pathway. In a group of 72 unoperated adults aged 25 years or older followed for 8 years, all but 5 patients were initially in New York Heart Association functional class I or II.447 Atrial arrhythmia was the most common clinical presentation in 51%, with flutter and fibrillation accounting for about half of these. Preexcitation was present in 44% on electrocardiogram. Onset of atrial arrhythmia commonly causes patients to progress to a worse functional class. Occasionally, adults present with untreated severe disease manifested by severe exercise intolerance, cyanosis, low cardiac output due to right heart failure, hepatic and renal compromise, and uncontrolled atrial and ventricular arrhythmias. Sudden death occurs and is associated with arrhythmias. Overall prognosis is poor in this subset of patients (Fig. 54.44).

Adults with previously diagnosed relatively mild disease will not have had prior surgery. These patients may present in adulthood with progression of cyanosis, progression of right heart failure symptoms, new-onset arrhythmias, or occasionally, paradoxical embolism.

Adults with prior surgery that included placing a bioprosthetic valve in the tricuspid position usually present with valve degeneration, manifested by a combination of predominant stenosis and some regurgitation. Adults with prior surgery that included tricuspid valve repair may also present with progressive tricuspid valve disease; however, recurrent regurgitation predominates, although stenosis after aggressive repair can also occur. Cyanosis in previously repaired patients is unusual because most surgical protocols involve closure of atrial-level shunts. Many repaired patients may have received a bidirectional superior cavopulmonary anastomosis as part of initial repair; upper body swelling, headache, and superior caval aneurysm may develop.

Some patients with complex disease have also received right ventricle to pulmonary trunk reconstructions in childhood. This will create the later requirement for conduit insertion as the native valve deteriorates or conduit replacements due to eventual conduit failure. A pulmonary valve problem, particularly stenosis, is

![](_page_71_Figure_6.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.44** Probability of death according to severity of tricuspid valve deformity in 72 adult patients with Ebstein anomaly. Tricuspid valve deformity was estimated by echocardiographic measurement of amount of displacement of septal leaflet from true valve anulus. Patients were then placed in Group 1 (mild deformity), Group 2 (moderate), or Group 3 (severe). (From Attie and colleagues.447)

likely to require intervention earlier than in other patients (e.g., Tetralogy) due to the regurgitant tricuspid valve.

New onset atrial arrhythmias are a common presentation as the right atrium dilates, and may precipitate quite significant clinical deterioration with a requirement for urgent cardioversion and early ablation. The onset of atrial tachyarrhythmias, although common, should be seen as one of the first signs of right heart failure. Some patients have left ventricular non compaction diagnosed in childhood and both left and right myocardial abnormalities create a risk for late sudden death or near miss events due to ventricular arrhythmias.

#### **Diagnosis**

The *echocardiogram* may show preexcitation due to a right-sided accessory bypass tract,448 prominent P waves, and right bundle branch block. The chest *radiogram* will show a variable degree of cardiomegaly due to right atrial enlargement, depending on severity of the morphologic and physiologic derangement. *Echocardiography* is diagnostic and in occasional cases is sufficient to proceed to surgery.449-451 It shows the structural alterations in the tricuspid valve, including apical displacement of the septal and posterior leaflets and the large abnormal anterior leaflet, the atrialized portion of the right ventricle, enlarged right atrium, and status of the atrial septum. It also provides physiologic information, including amount of tricuspid regurgitation, status of RV function, and presence and direction of atrial-level shunting. Other associated anomalies, such as pulmonary stenosis or regurgitation, ventricular septal defect, and left ventricular noncompaction, can also be identified. *Cardiac catheterization* may be indicated if there is unexpected ventricular dysfunction or concern for arteriosclerotic coronary artery disease; assessment includes measurement of ventricular end-diastolic pressure and imaging of the coronary arteries. It is also indicated to calculate pulmonary vascular resistance if a bidirectional cavopulmonary anastomosis is being considered as part of the repair. *Magnetic resonance imaging* has become the standard in assessing and following patients with Ebstein anomaly allowing demonstration of all pertinent structural alterations within the right ventricle and quantitative measurements of size and function of the RV and LV chambers as well as the RA which are useful in the decision to intervene and also in surgical planning. is capable of providing definitive information for diagnosis of Ebstein anomaly, including demonstration of all pertinent structural alterations discussed under echocardiography; additionally, it may provide quantitative functional information superior to that provided by echocardiography (Fig. 54.45).452

Formal *exercise testing* is be helpful. In addition to measuring functional capacity, pulse oximetry can demonstrate onset or worsening of cyanosis with exercise. This information may be helpful in determining the best operative approach to the patient, as well as response to therapy.

*Electrophysiologic* evaluation in all adults presenting. It is strictly indicated if there is evidence of arrhythmia on history, ECG or on Holter monitoring and is also required prior to a surgical tricuspid repair, in case there is an aberrant pathway or focus that needs ablating which may become inaccessible after the atrialized right ventricle is plicated and the anulus has had sutures inserted.

## **Natural History**

Adults with mild deformity of the tricuspid valve may be asymptomatic and have normal survival (see Fig. 54.44).447,453 They

![](_page_72_Picture_2.jpeg)

• Figure 54.45 Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) in 47-year-old man with unrepaired Ebstein anomaly. (A) Gated TI-weighted spin-echo four-chamber view demonstrating right atriomegaly and inferior insertion of tricuspid valve leaflets within atrialized right ventricular cavity. (B) Four-chamber view from cine-MRI demonstrating systolic flow dephasing (arrow) arising within right ventricular cavity, suggestive of Ebstein anomaly. (C) Sagittal oblique view showing use of a selective saturation pulse through right atrium parallel to atrial septum (curved arrow). Signal loss (arrow) indicates right-to-left shunting through a small atrial septal defect. (From Eustace and colleagues. 452)

typically have normal systemic arterial oxygen saturation, either without an atrial septal defect or with an atrial septal defect and left to right shunting. Patients with cyanosis from right to left shunting typically present earlier in life, partly because the cyanosis is obvious, but also because cyanosis is associated with a more severe form of the disease.

Luu and colleagues reviewed 51 adult patients in a Sydney ACHD program, starting at a mean age of 21 and followed for 20 years. During this time 35% had a documented SVT of which most had an ablation and just over 50% of those then had long-term freedom from SVT after their intervention. During the 21 years there were 20% who required an operation including several who already had replacements from childhood. Several others were repaired, all with excellent early outcomes. Overall survival of the cohort was 100% to the age of 40, 95% to the age of 50 and 81% to the age of 60 (Fig. 54.46).

## **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques used in adults with Ebstein anomaly are the same as those used in children. These are extensively discussed in Chapter 48.

A review of 595 operations on Ebstein patients from the STS Congenital Database (2002–2009, 82 centers) included 93 procedures in adults and looked at the variety of procedures performed.455 One third were reoperations and there were tricuspid valve repairs in 61% of these, replacement in 15%, a Fontan procedure in 5% and ASD closure in 3%. The operative differences between adult and pediatric procedures across the entire cohort are demonstrated in Table 54.20. They noted the rarity of cases, a mean of one case per institution per year. Interestingly, Dearani and colleagues have shown that in 294 adults (mean age 20 years), 12-year freedom from reoperation was identical in patients with initial valve repair versus replacement (Fig. 54.47).456 Both bioprosthetic and mechanical valves have been used when valve replacement is required; however, long-term survival is much better when bioprosthetic valves are used.457

The Cone Reconstruction, introduced by Jose Pedro da Silva in 1989, has achieved increasing success and widespread adaption by surgeons in the current decade. It is an ambitious operation but it not only corrects the tricuspid regurgitation with utilization of every piece of tricuspid leaflet tissue but it also returns the atrialized right ventricle back to the ventricular side of the anulus. Holst, Dearani and colleagues have now published the results of 235 Cone Repairs comparing the 134 children with 101 adults in the series with regard to techniques and results. Additional techniques to the standard Cone such as leaflet extension with a patch, Sebening stitch, anular band or neochordae insertion, were all deployed in similar proportions across the group (Table 54.21).458

The bidirectional Glenn is generally used sparingly in only about 5% of adults, but the ASD should be closed in all (present in 60%). In describing how to determine those adults who can have a Cone, Dearani notes that a very dilated tricuspid anulus over 6cm or more is a contraindication given the need for plication of the anulus down to 30mm and the proximity of the right

![](_page_73_Figure_7.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.47** Freedom from reoperation after initial tricuspid valve replacement compared with that after initial tricuspid valve repair in 294 patients (mean age 20 years at surgery) with Ebstein anomaly. *NS,* Not significant. (From Dearani and colleagues.)

coronary, which may get kinked in this situation and cause ischemia. Tricuspid bioprosthetic valve replacement is an acceptable option with reasonable durability as the patient reaches age 45.

Insertion of epicardial pacing wires should be considered part of the surgical strategy if there is any degree of preoperative heart block, as endocardial leads are not a good option through a bioprosthesis or a repaired valve.

Occasionally in mild cases of Ebstein anomaly, atrial septal defect may be the dominant structural anomaly. If there is a left-to-right shunt with both rest and exercise, catheter-based intervention and closure is appropriate. If cyanosis at rest is present, catheter-based intervention is contraindicated and surgery addressing the tricuspid valve as well as closing the ASD will achieve the best outcome and exercise tolerance (with a bidirectional

Careful discussion with regard to patient lifestyle and usual exercise capacity is necessary, and surgery may give the optimal long-term outcome for the younger adults in this category.

#### Results

Early mortality following initial surgery to correct Ebstein anomaly in adults ranges from 0% to 5%. 459-461 In the largest series of

![](_page_74_Figure_8.jpeg)

• Figure 54.48 Comparison of survival between patients with (red line) and without (blue line) superior cavopulmonary shunts (CPS) in 36 adults undergoing surgical correction of Ebstein anomaly. (From Al-Najashi and colleagues.)

adult patients (539 patients, mean age 24 years), early mortality was 5.9% (CL 4.9%–7.2%); however, this experience dates back to 1972. 462 For patients undergoing surgery between 2001 and 2006 in this series, early mortality was 2.7%. Twenty-year survival was 71%. Preoperative risk factors for overall mortality were left or right ventricular systolic dysfunction, increased hemoglobin, male sex, RVOT obstruction, and hypoplastic pulmonary arteries.

In another series of 40 patients undergoing initial surgical correction at a mean age of 41 years, early mortality was 5% (CL 1.7%-11%), and 5-year survival was 84%. 463 Survival was similar for patients receiving a concomitant superior bidirectional cavopulmonary anastomosis (23/40) and those who did not (17/40) (Fig. 54.48). Importantly in this study, the decision to include the cavopulmonary anastomosis was based on preoperative assessment of high operative risk (based on tricuspid valve and RV characteristics), and thus, the authors inferred that the cavopulmonary anastomosis had a beneficial effect on survival. Chauvaud and colleagues prospectively assigned high-risk patients to two groups, those receiving a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis and those who did not. 464 They showed a substantial early survival advantage for the group receiving the cavopulmonary anastomosis. Marianeschi and colleagues also showed that use of the superior bidirectional cavopulmonary anastomosis reduced the need for tricuspid valve replacement.<sup>119</sup>

In a study of outcomes for the Cone Repair, survival in the 101 adults was 98.9% at 2 and 4 years and 92.8% at 6 and 8 years. There were several early reoperations (5.9%) in the overall group, some of which resulted in valve replacement, but this improved with experience over the 8 years of the audit. 458

Functional status, substantially below normal in unrepaired adults, improves following surgery, as measured both by subjective patient response and objective exercise testing. 124,465 Hetzer, in his review of 68 patients with 13.2 years of mean follow-up time showed a significant improvement in NYHA class and improvement in formal exercise tolerance testing. Displacement tissue doppler imaging was obtained on echocardiography before and late after surgery to evaluate the performance of the right and left

ventricle and showed there was a significant improvement in ventricular strain after surgery. In this cohort there was 92% freedom from reoperation at 20 years and 93.7 and 91.3% survival at 5 and 20 years respectively.

Echocardiographic follow-up after the Cone Procedure in the paper from Holst demonstrated an early deterioration in RV function as measured by apical fractional area change, but later improvement at an average of 2.6 years of follow-up, although the results had not returned to the preoperative baseline. Longer follow-up time is needed on the Cone Procedure before we can be sure of its impact on the long-term function of the right and left ventricles.

Khositseth and colleagues reported on 83 Ebstein patients (mean age 28 years) with preoperative indication for concomitant arrhythmia surgery.106 A variety of surgical arrhythmia procedures were performed, including ablation of accessory pathways, perinodal ablation for atrioventricular nodal reentrant tachycardia, and the Maze procedure for atrial flutter or fibrillation. At a mean follow-up of 34 months, there was no recurrence of accessory pathway tachycardia or reentrant tachycardia, and a 25% recurrence of atrial flutter or fibrillation. Early mortality for concomitant arrhythmia procedures was 4.8%.

Freedom from reoperation after bioprosthetic tricuspid valve replacement was 81% at 15 years in a series of 158 patients (mean age 19 years at surgery).466 Although numbers are small the further replacement of the degenerating tricuspid valve bioprosthesis (included in some general adult series of TVR) has a moderately increased mortality risk and risk of complete heart block requiring pacemaker. Renal and liver dysfunction from chronically elevated venous pressure may complicate the surgery and prolong recovery.

## **Indications for Operation**

In unrepaired adult patients, cyanosis is an indication for surgical intervention. Progressive cardiomegaly due to tricuspid regurgitation, with right atrial and ventricular enlargement, is a relative indication for surgery, even if there are no symptoms. The natural history of the disease suggests that development of atrial flutter or fibrillation is likely if these findings are present. New onset of atrial arrhythmias in previously asymptomatic patients with relatively mild structural disease is an indication for surgery. Any patient with important symptoms or reduced functional capacity should undergo surgery. These specific indications are no different from those in younger patients with Ebstein anomaly, described in Chapter 48.

In adult patients with prior repair, the preceding indications all apply. Progressive regurgitation of a previously repaired valve has the same sequelae as an unrepaired regurgitant valve. Patients with prior tricuspid valve replacement will eventually present with a degenerated bioprosthesis. Most commonly, stenosis will predominate, but mixed stenosis and regurgitation is almost always present. Occasionally, regurgitation will predominate. Predominant prosthetic valve regurgitation presents like native valve regurgitation, and indications for intervention are similar. Predominant prosthetic valve stenosis is an indication for surgery if the mean gradient is 10 mmHg or greater or if symptoms of reduced functional capacity, peripheral edema, or hepatic congestion are evident.

In adult patients with prior tricuspid repair, repeat valve repair is indicated if regurgitation recurs, but there is a moderate chance of replacement second time round. After the bioprosthesis degenerates, a further replacement is necessary once there are signs of right heart failure and to prevent important liver or renal damage. For the occasional high risk surgical candidate there is now an interventional option of a valve in valve procedure, useful for late presentations with renal or hepatic failure where surgical mortality risk may be very high.

## **SECTION XV: CORONARY ARTERY PROBLEMS RELATED TO CONGENITAL HEART DISEASE**

## **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of congenital coronary artery anomalies are described in Chapter 38. Presentation of coronary artery problems related to congenital heart disease in the adult may be as newly diagnosed primary disease or previously diagnosed primary disease with benign physiology (primary congenital coronary anomalies), or secondary disease which follows surgery for other congenital heart anomalies. Coronary artery problems in the adult may also result from sequelae of acquired pediatric diseases like Kawasaki disease, or as a result of external pacing leads placed in childhood tightening with somatic growth and constricting an adjacent coronary vessel, in otherwise structurally normal hearts.467

#### **Morphology**

Coronary artery problems related to congenital heart disease in the adult are uncommon and can be categorized into two main groups. One category includes the primary congenital coronary anomalies. The most important of these are coronary artery arising from the wrong aortic sinus, coronary artery arising from the pulmonary artery, and coronary artery fistula. These are discussed in detail in Chapter 38. A more complete listing of coronary artery anomalies is shown in Box 54.1. 468 Table 54.22 lists the 62 anomalies (1.2%) found in one study of 5100 coronary angiograms. The mean age of the patients was 65 years and during a 5-year follow-up period survival was 83% and 71% of all cardiac events and 100% of all deaths occurred in those with the severe anomalies often with superimposed atherosclerotic coronary disease.

The second category of coronary artery problems related to congenital heart disease encompasses otherwise normal coronary arteries that are put at risk, either as a result of surgical procedures used to repair other congenital heart anomalies (e.g., arterial switch operation, Ross operation) or as the natural progression of certain congenital heart diseases (e.g., supravalvar aortic stenosis). Accelerated obstructive arteriosclerotic coronary artery disease, which can develop in patients with left-sided obstructive problems such as aortic coarctation, will not be discussed further in this section.

In patients with prior aortic root surgery or coronary transfers, coronary obstruction is usually ostial or very proximal. The early manipulation and translocation of coronary arteries can result in later scarring, malposition, torsion or tension, and then to hypoplasia and even occlusion. This may be aggravated by the development of aortic sinus dilation, commonly seen after the Ross or Arterial switch or the Norwood procedure. Kinking or flattening of the proximal coronary as it stretches over the dilated sinus, may cause deterioration in coronary perfusion over time.

Finally, in adult cases of supravalvar stenosis, the obstruction may involve the leaflet opening into the sinus, the ostium itself,

or the coronary stem, if a more diffuse arteriopathy is present (see Section IX of this chapter).

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

## **Presentation**

Patients with coronary anomalies of any kind may present with classic signs and symptoms of coronary insufficiency, including angina, ventricular arrhythmia, sudden death, left ventricular dysfunction, or mitral regurgitation. However many of these anomalies may be more occult if the coronary insufficiency is less severe or develops slowly and allows coronary artery collaterals to develop. In the current era many minor coronary anomalies are found incidentally, for instance on the investigation of a presyncopal episode in a young adult, and require investigation to determine their importance before sporting activities can be allowed and correct management can be decided.

Patients with coronary artery fistulas present with chest pain or dyspnea in 71% of cases, and with either fatigue, palpitations, heart failure, or syncope in another 20%.125 Only 9% are asymptomatic. Symptoms are much more likely in adults than in children, who are asymptomatic 79% of the time. In adults, spontaneous closure occurs in 3%, rupture in 4%, and aneurysm in 14%. Endocarditis has been reported. Fistulas are single in 74% to 90% of cases.

Anomalous origin of a coronary artery from the pulmonary trunk presenting as newly diagnosed disease in the adult will

rarely involve the left main coronary artery because the natural history does not allow asymptomatic survival unless there is associated stenosis of the anomalous ostium or pulmonary hypertension. When it does present in the adult, severe left ventricular failure is present (see Chapter 38).125 Isolated anomalous origin of the left anterior descending, circumflex, or right coronary artery is more likely to present in the adult (Figs. 54.49 and 54.50).

The presentation of a coronary artery arising from the wrong aortic sinus is reviewed in detail in Chapter 46. Both the left main coronary artery arising from the right aortic sinus and the right coronary artery arising from the left aortic sinus can be associated with ischemia, and particularly with sudden death in young adult athletes either during or immediately after exertion.125

Kawasaki disease acquired in childhood may result in chronic progressive degenerative changes in the coronary arteries, leading to coronary ectasia, aneurysm formation, stenosis, and occlusion. Aneurysms have a tendency to thrombose and to develop stenosis at both their proximal and distal ostia.

#### **Diagnosis**

The electrocardiogram may show changes where there is a history of infarction but in many cases it will be normal and this does not rule out an important coronary lesion. Echocardiography and cardiac catheterization are indicated to establish etiology. Echocardiography is the standard imaging modality to determine the position of the coronary origins, and their initial pathway, with the help of color doppler to determine direction of flow. Function
