of the myocardium and valves is also assessed. Computed tomography coronary angiography is the next modality commonly adapted to better define the coronary origins and pathway particularly for more distal lesions or after aortic root surgery where there is root dilation or distortion. MRI angiography may be chosen as an alternative to CT.

The more invasive catheter angiography gives more detail of the coronaries and is an important confirmation of the abnormality before surgery, for instance in the late diagnosis of anomalous circumflex coronary from a pulmonary trunk (see Fig. 54.49). Magnetic resonance imaging and nuclear perfusion scanning is useful to demonstrate the presence of myocardial fibrosis or ischemia, and this is helpful if the area correlates with the location of a coronary anomaly. (see Fig. 54.50).

If the anomaly involves origin of a coronary artery from the wrong aortic sinus, exercise stress testing is not indicated or warranted in those for whom there is a history of ischemia or infarction (often the left coronary arising from the right sinus), for whom a surgical repair is already indicated. If the anomaly involves origin of a coronary from the wrong sinus in a patient with minor or no symptoms (often the right coronary arising from the left sinus), then exercise stress testing or other tests designed to provoke ischemia may be helpful if they are positive in the area that corresponds to the anomalous coronary supply. However a negative test does not entirely rule out the possibility of a future sudden event. Decision-making in this group is difficult and depends on a number of factors which are well set out in Table 54.23 of the 2018 AHA/ACC Guideline for the Management of Adults With Congenital Heart Disease and include age, detailed anatomy of the ostium and proximal course, type of anomalous origin, exercise level, tests results of ischemia, and symptoms (Table 54.23).15

Asymptomatic adults with known history of previous surgery or another condition that puts the coronary arteries at risk should be managed with a high index of suspicion. Patients who have had arterial switch surgery should undergo noninvasive testing for myocardial ischemia every 3 to 5 years.12 This recommendation is based on the established occurrence of coronary artery occlusion and stenosis found incidentally at cardiac catheterization after this operation.127 If noninvasive testing is positive, further coronary imaging as described earlier in this section is indicated. Asymptomatic patients who have undergone a Ross procedure should have imaging of their coronary arteries at least once during adult life.12 Often this can be timed to be part of the preoperative evaluation in preparation for RVOT conduit revision. Asymptomatic patients with a history of repaired or unrepaired supravalvar aortic stenosis should undergo noninvasive testing for myocardial ischemia as adults every 2 years, based on the known association of diffuse arteriopathy and known occurrence of coronary events associated with this anomaly.12,15

## **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques used to treat adults with primary congenital anomalies of the coronary arteries are described in Chapter 38. In particular, Brothers and colleagues in their Expert Consensus Guidelines: Anomalous aortic origin of a coronary artery, have included descriptions and diagrams of several operative techniques pertinent to adults.488 Figs. 54.51–54.52 show the unroofing technique, Fig. 54.53 the reimplantation of the right coronary, and Fig. 54.54 the coronary osteoplasty for a stenotic left mainstem. They comment that coronary artery bypass grafting, although very familiar to many adult surgeons, is not the procedure

## **TABLE 54.23 Factors That May Relate to the Clinical Importance of AAOCA and Risk of SCD**

Age AAOCA is more commonly invoked as the cause of SCD in patients ,35 years of age than in patients .35 years of age, in whom atherosclerotic coronary disease becomes a more prevalent cause. However, death has been attributed to AAOCA in patients of all ages; there does not seem to be an age beyond which the AAOCA may not be relevant, even in the setting of atherosclerotic coronary disease and other concomitant conditions.470,471

Anatomy of coronary ostium and proximal coronary course

Slit-like/fish-mouth-shaped orifice, acute angle takeoff, intramural course, interarterial course and hypoplasia of the proximal coronary artery have all been proposed as reasons for symptoms, ischemia and SCD in patients with AAOCA. The slit-like orifice is more commonly seen in anomalous right coronary artery arising from the left sinus. Each of these anatomic findings offers a pathophysiologic mechanism for intermittent ischemia, particularly at times of high cardiac output and/or increased aortic wall tension, such as during exercise.472-475

Anomalous origin Left coronary artery arising from the right cusp is less common than the right coronary artery arising from the left cusp but is more often found in autopsy series of SCD.470,476,477 This suggests that anomalous origin of the left coronary artery from the right cusp is more likely to cause SCD than anomalous origin of the right coronary artery from the left cusp. This may be due either to anatomic features that make anomalous aortic origin of the left coronary artery prone to coronary compromise or because a larger proportion of myocardium is supplied by the left coronary artery, or both.

Exercise Autopsy series suggest most patients die during, or in close temporal association with, exercise.476,478,479

Ischemia Autopsy series demonstrate myocardial fibrosis in a significant number of patients whose deaths were attributed to AAOCA, particularly in patients with anomalous left coronary artery arising from the right cusp.479 Surgical series describe patients with ischemia or MI before surgical repair in the absence of other CAD, suggesting a relation of the coronary anomaly to the ischemia.480 This suggests that had perfusion imaging been obtained before SCD, ischemia would have been found in such patients.481,482 However, other data indicate that a normal stress test does not preclude a SCD event, with the proviso that most of those studies used only stress ECG, rather than the more sensitive and specific modalities of nuclear perfusion imaging or stress echocardiography. In addition, postoperative studies have shown that ischemia may be found after surgical repair in the distribution not supplied by the abnormal coronary artery and may not persist on repeat testing.483

Symptoms In autopsy and surgical series, a significant number of patients reported cardiovascular symptoms, including before SCD events.478,484-487 Symptoms are more commonly reported in patients in whom the left coronary artery arises from the right sinus. Surgical series have described improvement in symptoms after surgical repair.472,476,478,479,484,485

*AAOCA*, anomalous aortic origin of the coronary artery; *CAD*, coronary artery disease; *ECG*, electrocardiogram; *MI*, myocardial infarction; *SCD*, sudden cardiac death. Reproduced from Stout KK, Daniels CJ, Aboulhosn JA, et al. 2018 AHA/ACC guideline for the management of adults with congenital heart disease: executive summary: a report of the American College of Cardiology/American Heart Association Task Force on Clinical Practice Guidelines. *J Am Coll Cardiol.* 2019;73(12):1494-1563.

of choice here where the competitive flow may affect the graft durability and ligation of the proximal coronary may not allow sufficient flow into a large distal system and predispose the patient to sudden death in the event of graft closure. Coronary origin stenting by the interventional cardiologists may be useful in situations where the risks of surgery are prohibitive, but concerns about intimal ingrowth and durability make it inadvisable in fit adults who could safely undergo an origin repair.

Surgical techniques used for coronary artery obstruction in adult patients who have undergone surgery for a congenital heart defect are described in Chapter 9. For those patients who have significant dilation of the aortic sinuses after a Ross procedure or Arterial switch, with acquired coronary compromise, consideration should be given to remodeling the aortic root, with valve preservation and reimplantation of the coronary buttons into better positions.

#### **Results**

Data on early mortality for repair of coronary anomalies in the adult population is limited, but the result in combined pediatric and adult series are excellent, with very low morbidity/mortality risk and excellent intermediate survival. In those who are symptomatic the ischemic symptoms have usually resolved. However, the stress testing can sometimes remain positive, and there have been occasional late sudden deaths reported, which indicates we have not yet proven that for the less risky lesions, surgery should always be recommended. In some clinical situations it may be best to recommend long-term limitation to noncompetitive sports.488 Early mortality for ligation of fistulas, or interventional closure, ranges from 0%–4%, with some patients reported to have aggravation of ischemic symptoms rather than resolution.15

## **Indications for Operation**

Surgical correction is indicated for all coronary arteries arising from the pulmonary trunk, for all symptomatic patients with coronary arteries arising from the wrong sinus, and for asymptomatic patients age 50 years or younger with coronary arteries arising from the wrong sinus if the coronary artery passes between the great arteries, is intramural, or has evidence of angiographic narrowing (see Chapter 38). The 2018 AHA/ACC Guideline for the Management of Adults With Congenital Heart Disease include a useful algorithm for those with anomalous origin of the coronary artery (Fig. 54.55).15 Coronary artery fistula closure, either by surgery or by catheter-based intervention, is indicated for patients with symptoms attributable to the fistula, regardless of the size of the left-to-right shunt, and for asymptomatic patients if the fistula causes a large left-to-right shunt. Catheter-based closure of a coronary artery fistula can be effective130,489 and is indicated if morphologic

In a literature review of coronary artery fistulas in adults covering 1993 and 2004, 107 fistulas were identified.490 Definitive management was noted in 67%, with 38% closed surgically, 24% medically managed, and 5% closed by percutaneous embolization.

If coronary abnormalities are detected in patients with a history of supravalvar aortic stenosis, management will depend on the nature and severity of the abnormality. Obstructive lesions should be managed like other more common arteriosclerotic lesions, with either coronary artery bypass grafting or catheterbased dilation or stenting (see Chapter 9). Ostial reconstruction for ostial stenosis or occlusion as recommended in children is possible in adults and may remain an attractive option to the congenital surgeon familiar with coronary ostial techniques.

Indications for intervention for coronary obstructive lesions identified in patients who have undergone an arterial switch operation or Ross procedure are:

- Any obstructive lesion associated with symptoms of ischemia
- Asymptomatic lesions with 50% or greater luminal narrowing
- Asymptomatic lesions with demonstrable flow disturbance

Intervention may be by surgery or catheter, depending on the position and nature of the lesion, and the patients age (see Chapter 9).491-493

Surgical management of coronary involvement in adults with prior Kawasaki disease has limited data and decision making is complicated and varies according to the specific lesion. If ischemia is present, bypass grafting or catheter balloon dilation and stenting may be indicated. Large saccular aneurysms may be asymptomatic but carry a risk of rupture, thrombosis, and occlusion of adjacent coronary arteries. Aneurysm resection and coronary reconstruction or bypass grafting may be indicated.

In the current era, as more of these adults are diagnosed, and higher levels of exercise are being adapted by many younger and older adults, the threshold for advising surgery, rather than restriction of sporting activities and acceptance of any risk of a sudden event, has been impacted.

## SECTION XVI: FUNCTIONAL UNIVENTRICULAR HEART

#### **Definition**

Definition and details of single-ventricle physiology are described in Chapter 52.

## Morphology

A spectrum of morphologic entities falls under the category of single-ventricle physiology. The most common is hypoplastic left heart syndrome (see Chapter 51), followed by tricuspid atresia (see Chapter 52) and then by less common entities, such as double inlet ventricle and common ventricle (see Chapter 52), unbalanced atrioventricular septal defect (see Chapter 32), certain forms of Ebstein anomaly (see Chapter 48), and certain forms of pulmonary atresia with intact ventricular septum (see Chapter 36). In addition, some patients are managed as having single-ventricle physiology even though their structural anomalies are characterized by two well-formed ventricles and two well-formed atrioventricular valves, because the surgical reconstruction that would be required to septate such hearts is either impossible or inadvisable. An example would be some forms of double outlet right ventricle with noncommitted ventricular septal defect (see Chapter 45). The palliated physiologic state of all survivors dictates that this small group of patients will demand a disproportionately large amount of resources in the adult congenital heart disease program.

Although most infants born with single ventricle physiology will not survive childhood without intervention, there may be a small proportion of adults, especially in developing countries who present with newly diagnosed disease, previously diagnosed disease with benign physiology, or previously diagnosed disease thought to be too complex for intervention. Only 13 adult patients with mixed circulation were evaluated over a 10-year period in one large adult congenital referral center. The great majority of patients will present in adulthood with secondary disease following prior surgery. They will have three basic physiologic states:

- Palliation with completely mixed circulation and controlled pulmonary blood flow (mixed circulation with systemicto-pulmonary artery shunt or pulmonary trunk band, or unoperated, balanced circulations)
- Palliation with partially separated circulation (superior cavopulmonary anastomosis, or Glenn, circulation)
- Palliation with separated circulations (Fontan circulation)

The general goal in infancy and childhood is to achieve Fontan circulation in all patients with single-ventricle physiology. Some patients will not meet criteria required to undergo the Fontan and will reach final palliation with a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis. Still others will not meet criteria for a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis and will reach final palliation with a systemic-to-pulmonary artery shunt or a pulmonary trunk band.

A deeper level of complexity exists in this patient population. Patients with Fontan circulation may have an atriopulmonary Fontan, a lateral tunnel Fontan, or an extracardiac conduit

Fontan. Additionally, the circulations may not be fully separated if a fenestration was created as part of the Fontan operation. Patients with Glenn circulation may have a bidirectional cavopulmonary connection or a unilateral cavopulmonary connection, and may or may not have additional sources of pulmonary blood flow through a surgically created systemicto–pulmonary artery shunt or pulmonary trunk band, or through a congenitally stenotic pulmonary valve. Patients with completely mixed circulation may have a single source of pulmonary blood flow through a shunt or band, or may have multiple sources of pulmonary blood flow through various combinations of shunts and bands. All these variations have particular importance in the adult with respect to the nature of sequelae that will be encountered.

As a further layer of complexity, single-ventricle patients with any of these palliated states may have had arch reconstruction, repair of anomalous pulmonary veins, valve repairs or replacements, or subvalvar ventricular outflow tract muscle resections. Some patients may have their morphologic tricuspid valve, pulmonary valve, and morphologic right ventricle functioning in the systemic circulation. All these factors will have management implications in the adult.

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

#### **Presentation**

Single-ventricle adults may undergo evaluation for surgical therapy for the following reasons:

- Structural problems such as arch obstruction, bulboventricular foramen obstruction, valve regurgitation, or pulmonary artery stenosis regardless of *mixed, partially separated*, or *separated circulation*
- Cyanosis in those without a completed Fontan

#### **Solutions include:**

- a. Systemic-to-pulmonary shunt or pulmonary trunk band revision (or rarely, initial placement) in *mixed circulation* patients
- b. Advancement of *mixed circulation* patients to *partially separated circulation* by performing a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis
- c. Advancement of patients with *partially separated circulation* to *separated circulation* with a Fontan operation
- d. Addition of a second source of pulmonary blood flow in cyanotic patients with a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis
- Atrial dilation, thrombosis and arrhythmias –requirement a Fontan conversion from atriopulmonary connection to extracardiac conduit with RA Maze
- Bradycardias requiring pacemaker surgery

Adult patients with *mixed circulation* can present in a variety of ways. Progressive cyanosis is common, especially in patients with a systemic-to–pulmonary artery shunt. Often accompanied by increasing exercise intolerance. Heart failure resulting from chronic cyanosis and volume overload is also common. This may be accompanied by onset of arrhythmias, atrioventricular valve regurgitation, and increasing exercise intolerance as well. Patients with chronic unrestricted pulmonary blood flow present with pulmonary hypertension with Eisenmenger physiology. Infective endocarditis may develop, either at the shunt or band site or at an abnormal valve. Paradoxical embolism may occur, resulting in cerebrovascular events or other end-organ dysfunction. Thrombotic complications may develop as a result of increased blood viscosity related to elevated hematocrit. Brain abscess is also described in this population.

Adult patients with *partially separated circulation*—those with a bidirectional superior cavopulmonary anastomosis typically present with progressive cyanosis as upper to lower body venovenous collaterals and pulmonary arteriovenous malformations develop. Because of the cyanosis and partial mixing of the circulations, all of the sequelae described for mixed circulation patients can also occur. Unless there is an additional source of pulmonary blood flow, superior cavopulmonary anastomosis patients will not have such a volume-loaded ventricle, and thus ventricular failure is less common than in mixed circulation patients, although their longstanding hypoxia will have caused quite significant aortopulmonary collateral circulation to have developed in the lungs.

Adult patients with *separated circulation*—those with a Fontan present differently. At baseline, they have greatly diminished lung function and aerobic capacity.131,495 Those with an atriopulmonary Fontan connection inevitably develop atrial dilation, which is associated with atrial arrhythmias, atrial thrombus with possible pulmonary embolism, and right pulmonary venous obstruction. Low cardiac output commonly develops in adult patients, related to the time since establishment of the Fontan connection, and is associated with exercise intolerance, protein-losing enteropathy, hepatic congestion and dysfunction, peripheral edema, pleural effusion and ascites, and plastic bronchitis. Obstruction in the Fontan pathway may accelerate these signs and symptoms, as may progressive elevation of pulmonary vascular resistance from chronic pulmonary emboli. Atrial shunting resulting in cyanosis is unusual; however, venovenous connection from the systemic circulation to the pulmonary veins may be a cause of cyanosis. Patients with lateral tunnel and extracardiac conduit Fontan connections do not develop atrial dilation and thus are less likely to develop atrial thrombus with chronic pulmonary emboli and atrial arrhythmias.496,497 Atrial arrhythmias, however, such as flutter, fibrillation, and sinus node disease leading to junctional rhythm, can occur and are an indication for ablation or Maze procedure and epicardial pacemaker insertion. Low cardiac output, and its sequelae develops more slowly in those with the extracardiac Fontan than it did for atriopulmonary Fontan connection patients.

#### **Diagnosis**

The main utility of the *electrocardiogram* (ECG) is to identify atrial and ventricular arrhythmias. Holter monitoring and electrophysiologic evaluation may be indicated based on the ECG or symptoms. The chest *radiograph* is particularly helpful in identifying and following pleural effusions in Fontan patients. Effusions suggest low cardiac output and failing Fontan circulation, and all of the sequelae associated with it, including protein-losing enteropathy.498 *Echocardiography* is the mainstay for evaluating and following single-ventricle patients. The usual intracardiac structural and functional evaluation of the ventricle, valves, aortic arch, and arterial and venous connections is performed. In Fontan patients, additional evaluation of the Fontan pathway is performed, looking for obstruction at the inferior caval and pulmonary artery ends, for thrombus and low flow with spontaneous contrast formation, for flow characteristics and severity of dilation of the inferior vena cava

Routine TTE may be unnecessary in a year when CMR imaging is performed unless clinical indications warrant otherwise.

CMR may be indicated for assessment of the long-term sequelae of Fontan palliation: thrombosis, right-to-left shunts (e.g., fenestration. intrapulmonary atrioventricular malformation), obstructive lesion, systemic atrioventricular valve dysfunction, ventricular size and function, collateral burden, and branch pulmonary artery obstruction. Baseline study is recommended with periodic follow-up CMR, with frequency of repeat imaging determined by anatomic and physiologic findings.

CCT may be used if CMR is not feasible and to evaluate cross-sectional imaging status-post stent therapy. CCT with contrast injection in Fontan patients can be misleading; therefore, it should be done only when clinically indicated and when it can be appropriately protocoled and interpreted. *ACHD*, adult congenital heart disease; *CCT*, cardiac computed tomography; *CMR*, cardiovascular magnetic resonance imaging; *CPET*, cardiopulmonary exercise test; *ECG*, electrocardiogram; *TTE*, transthoracic echocardiogram.

Reproduced from Stout KK, Daniels CJ, Aboulhosn JA, et al. 2018 AHA/ACC guideline for the management of adults with congenital heart disease: executive summary: a report of the American College of Cardiology/American Heart Association Task Force on Clinical Practice Guidelines. *J Am Coll Cardiol.* 2019;73(12):1494-1563.

and hepatic veins, and for the status of a fenestration if one is present.

*Cardiac catheterization* is indicated in any symptomatic singleventricle patient to assess a number of variables, including:

- Ventricular hemodynamics
- Pulmonary vascular morphology, pressure, and resistance
- Cardiac output
- Qp/Qs ratio
- Status of surgical shunts
- Bands and cavopulmonary connections
- Assessment of pulmonary arteriovenous malformations, venovenous abnormal connections, and systemic-to–pulmonary artery collateral formation
- Coronary arteries

*Cardiac catheterization* is a necessity in any single-ventricle patient with previously operated or unoperated mixed circulation, or partially separated circulation, who is being considered for Fontan completion surgery (see Chapter 52 for criteria for proceeding to the Fontan). It is also a necessity for any patient being evaluated for conversion from an atriopulmonary Fontan to either an extracardiac conduit or a lateral tunnel Fontan.

*Magnetic resonance imaging* (MRI) and *computed tomography* with angiography may be useful in single-ventricle patients, usually to augment the structural and functional echocardiographic evaluation, particularly in the pulmonary arteries and veins. MRI is also useful for functional evaluation of the ventricular myocardium. Unfortunately it is contraindicated in those adults with an epicardial pacing system, but CT is useful for 3D information and surgical planning. These adults Fontan survivors have for many years compensated for their chronic low cardiac output hence serial exercise testing is useful to detect gradual clinical changes and ascertain responses to both medical and surgical interventions (Table 54.24).15

## **Natural History**

The best prognosis is for patients with S, L, L double inlet left ventricle with moderate pulmonary stenosis. Survival into the sixth decade is possible.494

Ten-year survival after lateral tunnel and extracardiac conduit Fontan connection, the predominant Fontan operations performed over the last 20 years, is 90% or greater, much better than that observed after the atriopulmonary Fontan.499 Data from the Australia and New Zealand Fontan registry followed all 683 adult survivors and reported 90% survival rate at age 30 and 80% at age 40 years with these rates looking worse for the AP connection. However 20% had experienced a new arrhythmia, 6% required a pacemaker, 21% required a surgical reintervention and 70% had a thromboembolic event (Fig. 54.56 and Table 54.25).500 Another paper reports only early and late surgical and catheter reinterventions after the Fontan operation with two-thirds requiring reintervention by age 20 years, with pacemaker insertion being the commonest surgical procedure, and with any patient requiring a surgical procedure having an increased risk of late death (Table 54.26 and Table 54.27) 501

Survival in Fontan patients nevertheless is much better than the approximately 50% 20-year survival for patients with *mixed circulation* and *partially separated circulation.*12

The chronicity of the abnormal hemodynamic state associated with Fontan circulation leads to additional challenges for adults not generally considered in younger patients. Prevalence and severity of chronic lower extremity venous insufficiency are much greater than in the normal population.502 This has resulted in recommended close surveillance of the liver in the Fontan cohort, in particular to detect liver fibrosis, cirrhosis and hepatocellular carcinoma early as it may otherwise be asymptomatic.15 Atrial arrhythmias occur with increasing frequency in adults, noted in

## **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques, including systemic-to–pulmonary artery shunt, pulmonary artery band, bidirectional superior cavopulmonary anastomosis, and extracardiac conduit and lateral tunnel Fontan operations, are described in Chapter 52.

Therapeutic catheter-based procedures may be used to relieve various obstructions and to occlude abnormal venovenous connections, systemic-to–pulmonary artery collaterals, surgical shunts, and Fontan fenestrations.

The Maze procedure is modified as appropriate for patients with the variable morphologic features associated with the spectrum of single-ventricle anomalies. Fig. 54.57 shows the cryolesions for a right and left atrial Maze procedure applied to a patient with tricuspid atresia who is about to undergo an extracardiac conduit Fontan.

The technique for safe reentry in adult CHD patients is described in Overview Section. Many Fontan adults will have an excessive open heart return on bypass and this impairs visibility for intracardiac repairs and washes out cardioplegia thereby making the myocardium more vulnerable. For this reason it is safer to perform these reoperations at hypothermic temperatures and lower flows. Some parts of the operation may be done with a beating heart; for example once the atria are closed whilst inserting the extracardiac conduit in Fontan conversion. Adequate venting of the systemic ventricle is crucial until cardiac tone and ejection are reestablished.

and adults after the Fontan Procedure. *J Am Coll Cardiol.* 2018;71:1009-1017.

#### **Results**

With regards to performing a late Fontan: in the largest single-institution study, 132 adults (mean age 23 years) underwent the Fontan procedure between 1973 and 2001, with an early mortality of 8.3% (CL 5.8%–12%).506 This was similar to Fontan mortality in children over the same time period at the same institution. Early mortality improved in later years of the study. Late survival is

shown in Fig. 54.58, and causes of late mortality are shown in Table 54.28. Risk factors for late mortality included male gender, pulmonary artery pressure greater than 15 mmHg, and age greater than 30 years.

In the next largest series, Veldtman and colleagues reported 61 cases (median age 36 years) with an early mortality of 13% (CL 8.6%–19%); 15-year survival was 67%.507 In this study, when the Fontan group was compared with a group of adult single-ventricle patients without Fontan circulation, the Fontan group had better functional status, fewer arrhythmias, better ventricular function, and less atrioventricular valve regurgitation.

Podzolkov and colleagues reported an early mortality of 6.9% (2/29; CL 2.4%–16%) for the superior bidirectional cavopulmonary anastomosis in adults, and 8.3% for the Fontan in adults. Both values were similar to their outcomes in children.117

Early mortality after conversion of the atriopulmonary Fontan to the cavopulmonary Fontan ranges from 0% to 25%.14,120,121,498 In one of the largest series (containing both children and adults), Mavroudis and colleagues reported no early mortality (CL 0%–4.6%) and one transplant in 40 conversions and concomitant Maze procedures (mean age 19 6 9.0 years at conversion).102 At a mean follow-up of 2.5 years, functional status was improved, and there was one death and two additional transplants. In another study of 27 conversions in adults (mean age 30 years), there were two early deaths (7.4%; CL 2.6%– 17%). Both patients who died had liver cirrhosis and had been evaluated and turned down for transplantation. Functional status improved following conversion. Most patients in this series (78%) also had concomitant Maze arrhythmia surgery for atrial flutter or fibrillation, with late arrhythmia recurrence of 14% at 4.2-year follow-up.122

10 year outcomes of Fontan conversion from the Australia and New Zealand registry500 demonstrated the superiority of an earlier conversion with 39 patients in 6 institutions operated at a median age of 24 years. 36 patients had concomitant right atrial cryoablation. One center performed the operation earlier as judged by a more recent onset of atrial arrhythmias, better NYHA class and fewer antiarrhythmia agents. The center performing earlier

Cardiac transplantation following the Fontan operation is discussed in Chapter 21.

## **Indications for Operation**

In adults with *mixed circulation* (usually previously operated, but rarely with no surgical history) and unclear physiology, full evaluation, including cardiac catheterization, will determine whether criteria are met to advance to a state of *separated circulation*. Criteria for advancing to a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis are the same as those for infants and children (see Chapter 52). If these criteria are met, however, in contrast to the situation in young patients, a superior cavopulmonary anastomosis will rarely be adequate as the sole source of pulmonary blood flow, because

Reproduced from Poh CL, Cochrane A, Galati JC, et al. Ten-year outcomes of Fontan conversion in Australia and New Zealand demonstrate the superiority of a strategy of early conversion. *Eur J Cardiothorac Surg.* 2016;49(2):530-535.

of severe cyanosis. A secondary source, either from a new or existing systemic–pulmonary artery shunt or pulmonary trunk band, or from native pulmonary blood flow across a naturally stenotic outflow tract to the pulmonary arteries, will be required.

If the criteria are not met to advance to a separated circulation in the adult with *mixed circulation*, and the patient is cyanotic due to reduced pulmonary blood flow, then further surgical management with a systemic-to–pulmonary artery shunt, or possibly a pulmonary trunk band revision (if one is in place already), is indicated. If the patient is cyanotic because of pulmonary hypertension, evaluation for heart-lung transplantation is the only surgical option.

If pulmonary overcirculation is present and pulmonary vascular obstructive disease is not, then shunt revision or pulmonary trunk band placement or tightening is indicated as appropriate. Such patients can be further evaluated with catheterization approximately 6 months after shunt or band revision to assess criteria for advancing to a separated circulation. If pulmonary vascular obstructive disease is present in the overcirculated patient, the only surgical option is heart-lung transplantation.

In adult patients with *partially separated circulation*, if criteria are met at cardiac catheterization (see Chapter 52), advancement to a separated circulation with a completion Fontan operation is indicated. If the patient does not meet criteria for a Fontan and is symptomatic from cyanosis, creation of an additional source of pulmonary blood flow (usually a systemic-to–pulmonary artery shunt) is indicated. If pulmonary arteriovenous malformations are partially the cause of cyanosis, the shunt should be placed to direct the systemic flow primarily to the lung with the dominant malformations. Rarely, large isolated arteriovenous malformations can be closed using percutaneous catheter-based methodology.

In adult patients with *separated circulation*, surgical intervention may be indicated in several circumstances. Any significant structural cardiac problem or systemic arterial, pulmonary arterial, systemic venous, pulmonary venous, or Fontan pathway obstruction is an indication for surgical correction if catheter-based intervention is contraindicated or unsuccessful. In symptomatic patients with low cardiac output due to elevated pulmonary vascular resistance, a surgical fenestration may be indicated in selected cases. Adult Fontan patients who are fully or nearly fully saturated, however, may not tolerate the increase in cyanosis that attends fenestration. All adult atriopulmonary Fontan patients with acceptable ventricular function and low pulmonary vascular resistance should be considered for conversion to an extracardiac conduit or lateral tunnel Fontan connection. Conversion is strictly indicated if atrial flutter or fibrillation, pulmonary venous compression, atrial thrombus, or structural problems such as obstruction in the Fontan pathway are present.120,121

In all single-ventricle patients with severe ventricular failure, regardless *of mixed, partially separated*, or *separated circulation*, and normal pulmonary vascular resistance, the only surgical option is heart transplantation134 (See Chapter 21).

If atrial flutter or fibrillation is present in single-ventricle patients undergoing surgery to correct structural problems, to advance to a more separated circulation, or to convert from an atriopulmonary to a cavopulmonary Fontan, a Maze procedure or one of its modifications is indicated as a concomitant procedure.121

Bradycardia or junctional rhythms can impact cardiac output significantly in single ventricle patients. Addition of atrial epicardial pacing is a useful maneuver to improve symptoms. Biventricular or LV apical epicardial pacing should be utilized to improve ventricular synchrony if there is compete heart block.

## **SECTION XVII: OTHER ANOMALIES RARELY REQUIRING SURGERY IN THE ADULT**

## **Isolated Pulmonary Stenosis**

#### **Definition**

Isolated pulmonary stenosis is a common congenital heart anomaly; however, it rarely requires surgery in the adult.508 The reasons for this are that the valve obstruction is often mild and self-limited, and percutaneous catheter-based valvotomy is often effective in those who need treatment.

## **Morphology**

Two common morphologic subtypes of isolated pulmonary stenosis are recognized. The predominant type is the doming valve with three fused commissures but underlying well-developed valve cusp tissue. The less common type (≈20%) is the dysplastic valve, defined by three cusps with fully formed commissures but thickened immobile myxomatous cusp tissue.508 Dysplastic valves are often accompanied by narrowing of the anulus and subvalvar or supravalvar stenosis. This type of valve is also associated with Noonan syndrome.138 Occasionally, isolated pulmonary valve stenosis will be caused by a unicuspid or bicuspid valve.

## **Technique of Operation**

Because surgery for pulmonary stenosis in the current era is relegated to a second-choice role behind balloon valvotomy, most patients presenting for surgery will have unfavorable morphology and will likely require pulmonary valve replacement. The occasional patient with a doming valve that does not respond to catheter-based balloon dilation, and the occasional patient with a doming valve who is undergoing cardiac surgery for another anomaly, are candidates for surgical commissurotomy. Most surgical cases, however, have either dysplastic morphology or one or more associated conditions, such as anular hypoplasia or valve regurgitation, and will require valve replacement. Occasionally, a dysplastic valve without regurgitation and with adequate anular size will respond to surgical valvuloplasty involving shaving of the thickened cusps.

Currently, the replacement option of choice is a bioprosthetic valve placed orthotopically. It may be necessary to patch augment the anular and supravalvar component of the RVOT or to resect muscle in the subvalvar component of the RVOT as well. Surgical reduction of the pulmonary trunk may be considered when there is associated marked poststenotic dilation of the pulmonary trunk, but this procedure is truly indicated only in the rare case when there are symptoms of compression of other mediastinal structures. Dissection, true aneurysm formation, and rupture of the pulmonary trunk are not described when pulmonary artery pressure is normal, and these potential complications should not be of concern.

The typical doming valve is managed reasonably well by catheter intervention in the adult. Some patients may have residual infundibular stenosis following balloon valvuloplasty, but that tends to regress over time.509

#### **Results**

Balloon valvotomy (BPV) for the doming valve is currently the first line of therapy. In an earlier series of 40 patients (age range 18–56 years), the gradient was reduced by catheter intervention but not eliminated (persistent gradient of 37 6 14 mmHg).139 All patients had mild or less regurgitation, and there was a tendency for subpulmonic gradients to regress at 24-month mean follow-up (Fig. 54.60). In a larger series, 132 patients (mean age 27.7 years; 95% CI 21.12–34.76) underwent BPV, with a mean follow up period of 5.7 years (95% CI 4.37–6.98). Intervention resulted in successful BPV (. 50% reduction in baseline gradient) in 124 patients (94%), and a partially successful BPV (20%–50% reduction in baseline gradient) in eight patients (6%). The mean RV systolic pressure was decreased from 90.6 mmHg (95% CI 84.90–96.33) to 50.9 mmHg (95% CI 47.17–54.61) (*P* , .001),

![](_page_89_Figure_10.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.60** Peak pulmonary systolic gradient (PG) by echocardiography from right ventricle to pulmonary artery before, immediately after, and at mean follow-up of 24 months after balloon pulmonary valvuloplasty in 32 adult patients. Bars represent mean 6 standard deviation. (From Kaul and colleagues.139)

and the pulmonary valve (PV) gradient was reduced from 70.7 mmHg (95% CI 65.12–76.22) to 29.0 mmHg (95% CI 25.83– 32.23) (*P* , .001). The gradient reductions were maintained after five and 10 years of follow up. Two patients (1.5%) developed restenosis that required repeat BPV. There was no referral for urgent surgery.510 Other studies with follow-up of up to 10 years after balloon dilation show similar findings.140,509,511-516 Dysplastic valves are not as effectively treated by catheter-based balloon valvotomy; however, it is reasonable to attempt catheter intervention as a first line treatment.508

Surgery (pulmonary valvotomy) for unselected cases of isolated pulmonary stenosis in the adult is performed rarely and is expected to have an early mortality approaching zero. Long-term survival and freedom from reoperation are also extremely favorable, however are dependent on presence of comorbidities. When valve replacement is necessary, long-term outcomes are similar to those for other patients with bioprosthetic valves in the pulmonary position.

## **Indications for Operation**

There are several indications for surgical intervention for isolated pulmonary stenosis:38

- After catheter-based balloon dilation with a residual peak echocardiographic Doppler gradient of greater than 60 mmHg in asymptomatic patients or a residual peak gradient of more than 50 mmHg in symptomatic patients
- In any patient with associated severe pulmonary regurgitation, anular hypoplasia, fixed subvalvar stenosis, supravalvar stenosis, or dysplastic valve morphology

• If other cardiac anomalies requiring cardiac surgery, such as coronary artery disease, ventricular septal defect, or tricuspid regurgitation, are present

If there is an associated atrial septal defect, combined catheterbased closure and balloon pulmonary valvotomy may be performed.517,518

## **Partial and Total Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Connection**

#### **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of total anomalous pulmonary venous connection (TAPVC) are described in Chapter 30.

## **Morphology**

It is rare for adults with TAPVC to survive into adulthood unrepaired and most such patients are likely to be seen in the underserved populations. A few isolated cases and small series are reported.519-521 Some individuals who survive to adulthood have unobstructed supracardiac form of the disease, large intraatrial communication, and low pulmonary vascular resistance with increased pulmonary blood flow, resulting in only mild cyanosis.522 However, many patients will develop pulmonary hypertension and develop increased cyanosis and exertional dyspnea.519

Partial anomalous pulmonary venous connection (PAPVC) is more likely to be right sided, associated with a large ASD and may cause gradual onset of pulmonary hypertension. Anomalous drainage of right sided upper pulmonary veins is frequently associated with superior sinus venosus ASD and that of the right inferior pulmonary veins with inferior caval type of sinus venosus defect.523

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

Clinical presentation is characterized by varying severity of cyanosis and signs and symptoms of right heart and pulmonary overcirculation. *Echocardiography* is diagnostic; however, cardiac catheterization is mandatory to assess the status of the pulmonary vascular bed and thereby operability. *Magnetic resonance imaging* (MRI) and *computed tomography* (CT) may be helpful.

## **Natural History**

Recurrent pulmonary vein stenosis presenting in adults with a history of infant repair of TAPVC is rare because recurrent obstruction following infant repair almost always occurs in the first few months after initial infant repair. Most patients requiring surgery in adulthood are therefore likely to have unrepaired TAPVC with varying degrees of pulmonary hypertension.

## **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques used to repair this anomaly in adults are the same as those used in infants (see Chapter 30). These hearts are significantly dilated due to long standing volume overload and so anterior transatrial approach to correction is preferred over the posterior approaches.

## **Results**

Surgical repair for TAPVC in adults can be achieved with low operative mortality and results in significant improvement in clinical status. In a series of 27 patients with a mean age of 19.7 years (range 15–48), the anatomic subtypes were supracardiac (*n* 515), cardiac (*n* 57), and mixed (*n* 55). Eleven patients had severe and the rest had moderate pulmonary arterial hypertension; six patients had significant RV dysfunction. A small interatrial communication was left open in four patients and in two patients, a fenestrated unidirectional valved patch was used to close the atrial septal defect. There were no early or late deaths. At follow-up of 3–127 months (mean 61.2), 20 patients were in NYHA class I and 7 in class II. Echocardiography showed normal right and left ventricular function in all patients, with reduction of pulmonary arterial pressures in 26 patients.519 In another series of 19 patients aged 18 to 38 years (mean 26.2), the anatomic variants included 10 patients with supracardiac TAPVC, 6 patients with connection to the coronary sinus, and 2 with TAPVC directly to the right atrium. Two patients died, one because of left atrial rupture and another from low cardiac output. At a mean follow-up of 7 years (range 2 to 24), Doppler echocardiography and right cardiac catheterization in 14 patients showed a significant reduction of the mean pulmonary arterial systolic pressure from 51.1 1/– 3.4 to 37.4 1/– 14.4 mm Hg (*P* , .01). The remaining three patients were in functional class I.520 In another series of 12 adult cases of isolated TAPVC, with an average age of 24.9 6 6.7 years (range 18 to 41 years), all patients suffered from right-sided volume overload with clinical manifestations varying from mild cyanosis to severe heart failure. All patients survived the operation, with good longer term (10 months to 14 years) outcomes.521

On long term follow-up of 22 1/– 8 years after repair of TAPVC in adulthood, atrial and ventricular arrythmias have been documented in up to 23 % of the survivors indicating a need for continued surveillance.524

## **Vascular Ring and Associated Arch Anomalies**

#### **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of vascular rings are described in Chapter 39. Vascular rings rarely present in adults but has been reported in the very elderly also.525 Most reported cases are single case reports.

## **Morphology**

In an earlier collective review of vascular rings in adults, the most common anomaly is double aortic arch (≈45%), followed by right aortic arch with anomalous left subclavian artery with ligamentum arteriosum (≈30%).526 However, in a large single center experience, the anatomic variants were right arch with aberrant left subclavian artery (SA) and Kommerell diverticulum (KD) in 22 (34%), left arch with aberrant right SA and KD in 20 (31%), double aortic arch in 12 (18%), right arch with mirror imaging and persistent ligamentum off KD in 7 (11%), and others in 4 (6%). Aneurysm of the Kommerell diverticulum is an important finding in adults and was seen in 12(18%) patients (Fig. 54.61).527 Circumflex aorta is a rare subtype that needs more complex surgical techniques for correction.

Dissection and rupture of aorta have also been reported in the setting of vascular ring.528

#### **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

Vascular rings in adults may be found incidentally529,530 or may present with symptoms of dysphagia lusoria or upper respiratory obstruction.526,527,531-535 It is more common for dysphagia to be the presenting symptom in adults than in children, although respiratory obstruction is still the most common presentation in adults (42% present with respiratory symptoms, 33% with dysphagia). An acute presentation may also occur in the presence of dissection or rupture of aorta.527,528 Diagnosis can often be made by chest *radiography*, contrast *esophagogram*, and *echocardiography*; however, MRI or CT including 3D reconstruction are critical, allowing more precise imaging of the vascular anomaly and also imaging of the vasculature relationship to adjacent structures, including the trachea and esophagus.

Pulmonary artery sling is rare in adults. The literature consists of case reports only. Most are asymptomatic, presenting as an incidental finding536,537; however, some present with esophageal or tracheal obstructive symptoms, or both.538 Diagnostic evaluation and indications for surgery are similar to those for vascular rings.

#### **Natural History**

Presentation in adulthood after vascular ring repair in childhood is rare although inadequate repair may lead to recurrence of

![](_page_91_Picture_10.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.61** (A) Right arch (R-Arch) with aberrant left subclavian artery (SA) off the Kommerell diverticulum (KD) with persistent ligamentum (PL), (B) left arch with aberrant right SA off the KD, (C) double aortic arch, (D) R-Arch with mirror imaging and PL off the KD, (E) R-Arch with aberrant left brachiocephalic artery and PL off the KD, and (F) pulmonary artery sling. Reproduced from *Saran N, Dearani J, Said S, et al. Vascular rings in adults: outcome of surgical management. Ann Thorac Surg. 2019;108:1217-1227.*

symptoms and need for reoperation.527 Ruzmetov and colleagues followed 183 childhood repairs for up to 35 years with no reports of late complications.539

## **Technique of Operation**

All rings can be approached effectively through a median sternotomy. However, the more traditional approach is through a lateral thoracotomy (see Chapter 39).527

Patients with aberrant subclavian artery and a Kommeral's diverticulum may need a 2 stage approach with a prior carotid to subclavian bypass followed by excision of the diverticulum.527 Surgical management of pulmonary artery sling is described in Chapter 39. In adults, in contrast to infants, tracheal reconstruction is usually not necessary.

#### **Results**

Repair of vascular ring in adults can be achieved with low mortality. In the Mayo Clinic series there was 1 early death in 65 patients. Morbidity included recurrent laryngeal nerve injury in 5 (8%) and chylothorax in 3 (5%). Symptomatic improvement occurred in 97%. Survival was 96.1%, 85.0%, and 73.4% at 1, 5, and 10 years, respectively. Dysphagia recurred in 9 (14%), which included 7 (11%) with esophageal dysmotility.527

#### **Indications for Operation**

Asymptomatic anomalies may be followed. Surgical repair is recommended in the presence of important symptoms.

#### **Cor Triatriatum**

## **Definition**

The definition and morphology of Cor Triatriatum are discussed in Chapter 31.

## **Morphology**

Morphology in adults is similar to that in children. Forty-one percent of the patients in the collective review had obstructive membranes and these patients tended to be younger and had more associated cardiac defects.540

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

Cor triatriatum or 'divided left atrium'541 has been reported infrequently in adults. A systematic review identified 171 cases in published literature since 2005540 Cor triatriatum dexter has been reported in adults, usually as an asymptomatic incidental finding.542,543 When symptoms are present, they mimic right heart failure.

Presentation of isolated cor triatriatum sinister in the adult is identical to that of mitral stenosis, including dyspnea, pulmonary hypertension, atrial fibrillation, right heart failure, and hemoptysis. In mild cases, presentation may be incidental. Diagnosis can be made by *echocardiography*; however, MRI and CT define the morphologic details with more clarity. *Cardiac catheterization* is indicated to assess the state of the pulmonary vasculature.

#### **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques are similar to those used in children (see Chapter 31). If chronic atrial fibrillation or flutter is present, a concomitant Maze procedure should be performed.

#### **Results**

In a collective review, 71/171 patients required intervention.540 Surgical repair has been performed without early mortality and with improvement in symptoms and reduction in pulmonary artery pressure.

#### **Indications for Operation**

Surgical repair for both types of cor triatriatum is indicated if symptoms are present or if other changes, such as thrombus formation, develop. In asymptomatic patients surgery, is indicated if there exists a significant gradient across the obstructive membrane.

#### **Sinus of Valsalva Aneurysm**

#### **Definition**

Definition, morphology, and basic physiology of sinus of Valsalva aneurysm are described in Chapter 37.

#### **Morphology**

Sinus of Valsalva aneurysms are uncommon in adults. Both ruptured and unruptured aneurysms are seen, rupture typically occurring between the ages of 20 and 40 years. The aneurysm occurs most frequently in the noncoronary and right coronary sinuses, but can occur in the left.544 Rupture of sinus of Valsalva aneurysm occurs most often into the right ventricle.545,546

VSD and aortic regurgitation are often associated especially in Asian or Oriental patients.546 A bicuspid valve is associated in about 9% of the cases.

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

Sinus of Valsalva aneurysms are generally asymptomatic until they rupture and are usually diagnosed at diagnostic imaging for other disorders (Fig. 54.62).544 However, they may cause problems from a mass effect without rupture, including coronary obstruction, aortic regurgitation, RV outflow obstruction, and ventricular tachycardia. Thrombus formation with subsequent embolism can also occur. When rupture occurs, it is usually into a right-sided chamber, creating an aortic to right atrial or RV fistula. Rupture may also cause cardiac tamponade. Rupture can present as an emergency with extreme hemodynamic instability. Patients with chronic rupture may present with retrosternal pain, palpitations, or features of heart failure. Unruptured Sinus of Valsalva aneurysms are often detected incidentally or during imaging of abnormal aortic valves, but may present with features of coronary ischemia.

#### **Natural History**

The natural history of small and moderate aneurysms is unknown, and the risk of rupture of large aneurysm, although well documented, is not quantified.

![](_page_93_Picture_2.jpeg)

**• Figure 54.62** Noncoronary cusp sinus of Valsalva aneurysm (all 3 coronary cusps dilated). *LCC*, left coronary cusp; *NCC*, noncoronary cusp; *RCC*, right coronary cusp. Reproduced from Weinreich M, Yu PJ, Trost B. Sinus of Valsalva aneurysms: review of the literature and an update on management. *Clin Cardiol.* 2015;38(3):185-189.

## **Technique of Operation**

Details of surgical repair are described in Chapter 37. The appropriate surgical procedure will vary from simple plication, to resection and sinus patch reconstruction, to aortic root replacement with coronary artery reimplantation.544 Ruptured aneurysms can be managed surgically or by transcatheter device closure. The aneurysm can be approached through the aorta, chamber of rupture or both. The defect is generally closed with a patch. An associated VSD is closed and aortic regurgitation if present is addressed.

Transcatheter device closure of smaller aneurysms is feasible and is increasingly being done.547

#### **Results**

In a series of 160 patients with mean age of (30612) years, the right coronary sinus origin of sinus of Valsalva aneurysm was found in 108 patients (67.5%), the noncoronary sinus in 51 (31.9%), and the left coronary sinus in one (0.6%). Rupture of SVA into the right ventricle was identified in 89 (55.6%) cases, the right atrium in 61 (38.1%), the left ventricle in 2 (1.3%) and no rupture in 8 (5.0%). Ventricular septal defect (VSD) and aortic regurgitation (AR) were found in 59 (37%) and 45 (28%) patients respectively. An approach via the involved chamber was used in 86 patients (54%), aortotomy in 8 (5%), and a combined approach in 66 (41%). Aortic valve replacement (AVR) was performed in 23/45 (51%) and aortic valvuloplasty (AVP) in 9/45 (20%) patients. Early mortality was 1.9% (*n* 5 3) with 2 late deaths. Actuarial survival was 94% at 10 years, and 88% at 20 years. New York Heart Association functional class improved significantly after surgery (P , 0.01).546

In another series of 55 adult patients (mean age 31 years), operative mortality was 3.6%. Actuarial survival was 93% at 10 years and 87% at 15 years, with .80% freedom from reoperations at 15 years.545

#### **Indications for Operation**

Treatment options are well defined. If surgery is required for aortic valve disease or any other cardiac problem, sinus of Valsalva aneurysms of any size should be addressed. If no other surgery is indicated, surgical correction of large and moderate-sized aneurysms is the best approach to prevent rupture and other sequelae. The recommended indications for surgical intervention in aortic root aneurysms also apply for sinus of Valsalva aneurysms. Small aneurysms should be followed carefully.

Ruptured sinus of Valsalva aneurysm should be treated urgently, often as an emergency if the rupture is acute and associated with hemodynamic compromise.

## **Double-Chambered Right Ventricle**

#### **Definition**

The definition and morphology of double-chambered right ventricle are described in Chapter 34. This anomaly is rare in adulthood in developed countries. Few series with multiple patients are reported in the literature,548-550 otherwise, there are only occasional individual case reports or mention of an occasional adult in series that include predominantly children.551,552 Larger series of adult patients are reported from countries with limited resources where this anomaly may go undiagnosed until adulthood.553

## **Morphology**

In adulthood, as in childhood, there are almost always associated structural anomalies. In one series of 50 patients, 96% had a ventricular septal defect.550

## **Clinical Features and Diagnostic Criteria**

Symptoms in adults are different from those in children. They include syncope, chest pain, and exertional dyspnea.548,549 New York Heart Association functional class ranges from II to IV in most patients. Diagnosis in the adult may be difficult. *Electrocardiography* (ECG) is nonspecific, showing evidence of RV hypertrophy, right atrial enlargement, and diffuse ST-wave changes. Transthoracic *echocardiography* often fails to make the diagnosis.548 The incorrect diagnosis is often tetralogy of Fallot, ventricular septal defect, or pulmonary valve stenosis. Transesophageal echocardiography may be more accurate. MRI has been reported to establish the diagnosis and may supplement echocardiography.554 *Cardiac catheterization* should be performed in all cases and establishes the diagnosis in all. It also should always be performed to assess coronary arteries and overall hemodynamics.

## **Technique of Operation**

Surgical techniques are similar to those used in children (see Chapter 34). Usually a transatrial–transpulmonary artery approach will be effective, with a formal right ventriculotomy unnecessary. Associated anomalies should be addressed as well.

#### **Results**

Early mortality should be less than 1%. All patients reported in the literature have survived, and follow-up in the reported series ranged from 15 to 270 months, with no late mortality, improved functional status, and a low occurrence of recurrent obstruction.548-550,552

## **Indications for Operation**

Surgery is indicated in the presence of symptoms, signs of myocardial strain or ischemia, or a gradient greater than 40 mmHg.
